Existence of weak solutions for Porous medium equation with a divergence type of drift term
Sukjung Hwang, Kyungkeun Kang, Hwa Kil Kim

Abstract
We consider degenerate porous medium equations with a divergence type of drift terms. We establish existence of nonnegative L q L^{q} -weak solutions (satisfying energy estimates or even further with moment and speed estimates in Wasserstein spaces), in case the drift term belongs to a sub-scaling (including scaling invariant) class depending on q q and m m caused by nonlinear structure of diffusion, which is a major difference compared to that of a linear case. It is noticeable that the classes of drift terms become wider, if the drift term is divergence-free. Similar conditions of gradients of drift terms are also provided to ensure the existence of such weak solutions. Uniqueness results follow under an additional condition on the gradients of the drift terms with the aid of methods developed in Wasserstein spaces. One of our main tools is so called the splitting method to construct a sequence of approximated solutions, which implies, by passing to the limit, the existence of weak solutions satisfying not only an energy inequality but also moment and speed estimates. One of crucial points in the construction is uniform Hölder continuity up to initial time for homogeneous porous medium equations, which seems to be of independent interest. As an application, we improve a regularity result for solutions of a repulsive Keller-Segel system of porous medium type.



2020 AMS Subject Classification  :  35A01, 35K55, 35Q84, 92B05

Keywords :  Porous medium equation, weak solution, Wasserstein space

Contents

1
Introduction

In this paper, we consider
porous medium equations
(PME) with divergence form of drifts in the form of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{t}\rho=\nabla\cdot(\nabla\rho^{m}-V\rho),&\text{ in }\ Q_{T}\\
\rho(\cdot,0)=\rho_{0},&\text{ on }\ \mathbb{R}^{d},\end{array}\right.\quad\text{ for }\ m>1,

(1.1)

on a cylindrical domain
Q_{T}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,T]
,
d\geq 2
,
0<T<\infty
,
where
\rho:Q_{T}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
and a vector field
V:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We note that neither the maximum principle nor the gradient flow structure is, in general, available for the equation (
1.1
) which has been rarely analyzed.
Nevertheless, for given nonnegative initial data, we establish mainly the existence of nonnegative weak solutions of (
1.1
) when the drift belongs to some sub-scaling classes (see Definition
1.1
).

First, we point out that
\Delta\rho^{m}\sim\nabla\cdot\left(\rho^{m-1}\nabla\rho\right)
which clearly shows the different tendencies depending on the constant
m
.
If
m=1
, then the diffusion is linear so (
1.1
) becomes the heat equation with drifts. When
m>1
, the equation is called
porous medium equations
(PME) in which we are interested in this paper. When
0<m<1
,it follows that
\rho^{m-1}
tends to infinity as
\rho
approaches to zero and we call such equations
fast diffusion equations
. These are important nonlinear evolution equations describing a number of physical applications: fluid flow, heat radiations in plasma, mathematical biology, and other fields. For a systematic presentation of the mathematical theories, we refer to monographs
[
52
,
53
,
21
,
24
]
and the references therein.

The equations (
1.1
) carry both nonlinear diffusion and drift that are useful in numerous places. For example, this type of PDEs represents a certain form of aggregation-diffusion (reaction-diffusion) equations that arises naturally in systems of interacting components and is used widely to describe pattern-formation phenomena and chemotaxis models in biological, chemical and physical systems.
For example, one can consider the porous medium equation with drift caused by fluid flow,

\rho_{t}-\Delta\rho^{m}+u\cdot\nabla\rho=0,\quad\text{ in }\ Q_{T}:={\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T],

where
u:Q_{T}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a divergence-free vector field.
Recently, there has been extensive research on so called enhanced dissipation effects for various PDEs with fluid flows for the linear case
m=1
(see e.g.
[
18
,
6
,
5
,
8
]
, etc). In particular, there are recent developments of suppression of blow-up for nonlinear equations such as Keller-Segel equations with external fluid flows (see e.g.
[
7
,
20
,
41
]
, etc).
Concerning free boundary regularity of (
1.1
), there are recent developments of
{\mathcal{C}}^{1,\alpha}
-regularity assuming enough smoothness on
V
and
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
in
[
39
,
40
]
and references therein for relevant works when
V=0
.
The large-time asymptotics and regularity of nonlinear diffusion equations with drifts are also analyzed as well: for example, the rate of decay to equilibrium of self-similar solutions in
[
15
]
, viscosity solutions, equivalent to weak solutions, and their asymptotic convergence of the free boundary to the equilibrium in
[
38
]
, and Hölder continuous solutions for aggregation-diffusion equations with singular interaction kernels in
[
58
]
. Compared to PME without drifts or linear diffusion-drift equations, fewer results are known for (
1.1
) because of difficulties of handling not only nonlinear diffusion but also drift term.

The drift may affect the well-posedness of diffusive parabolic equations. There are works showing counterexamples of lack of regularities under certain conditions on the drift, for example,
[
49
,
46
,
59
]
for (fractional) linear diffusion and
[
39
,
33
]
for porous medium equations.
From the perspective of critical conditions on
V
providing continuity of (
1.1
) (for example,
[
17
,
39
,
33
]
), we recall

V\in L^{q_{1},q_{2}}_{x,t}\quad\text{ where }\quad\frac{d}{q_{1}}+\frac{2}{q_{2}}\leq 1.

(1.2)

The condition (
1.2
) is also observed from the scaling invariant classes in
L^{\infty}
-sense. Moreover, the same conditions are well known from heat (linear) diffusion-drift equations (see e.g.
[
42
,
44
,
49
]
) and there is a connection with so called Prodi-Serrin-Ladyzhenskaya conditions concerning the existence of smooth solutions of the Navier-Stokes equations (see
[
43
,
47
]
).

One of motivations of this research has started from an observation that the nonlinear diffusion factor
m
yields different scaling invariance depending on conservation of
L^{q}
-norm in spatial variables.
Naturally, it follows a question of searching minimal conditions on
V
related to
m
, which allows us to construct weak solutions of (
1.1
) for a given
L^{q}
-initial data.
From this perspective, we investigate
the scaling invariant classes of
V
and
\nabla V
in
L^{q}
-sense for
q\geq 1
which turns out proper conditions for constructing
L^{q}
-weak solutions (see Definition
2.1
).
Heuristically, we look for conditions of
V
in terms of scaling invariant classes of solutions
for
L^{q}
-norm in spatial variables.
It turns out that the
L^{q}
-norm of
\rho
is preserved, if the following scaling is taken: for
q\geq 1
and
\kappa>0

\rho_{\kappa}(x,t)=\kappa^{d/q}\rho(\kappa x,\kappa^{\beta}t)\quad\text{and}\quad V_{\kappa}(x,t)=\kappa^{\alpha}V(\kappa x,\kappa^{\beta}t),

where

\alpha=q_{m,d}+1,\quad\beta=q_{m,d}+2\quad\text{for}\quad q_{m,d}:=\frac{d(m-1)}{q}.

We then see that
V_{\kappa}
is scaling invariant in a mixed norm
L^{q_{1},q_{2}}_{x,t}
, i.e. for
1\leq q_{1},q_{2}\leq\infty
, it holds
\left\|V_{\kappa}(x,t)\right\|_{L^{q_{1},q_{2}}_{x,t}}=\left\|V(x,t)\right\|_{L^{q_{1},q_{2}}_{x,t}}=\left\|\left\|V(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}_{x}}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}_{t}}

provided that

\frac{d}{q_{1}}+\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{q_{2}}=1+q_{m,d}.

We also note that similar condition of the scaling invariance for
\nabla V
is that
\nabla V\in L^{\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2}}_{x,t}
, where
\tilde{q}_{1}
and
\tilde{q}_{2}
satisfy

\frac{d}{\tilde{q}_{1}}+\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{\tilde{q}_{2}}=2+q_{m,d}.

Now we define the
scaling invariant
and
sub-scaling classes
of
V
and
\nabla V
in accordance with
q
and
m
.

Definition 1.1

Let
m,q\geq 1
and
q_{m,d}:=\frac{d(m-1)}{q}
. For constants
q_{1},q_{2},\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2}>0
, we define following spaces.

(i)

The scaling invariant classes of
V
and
\nabla V
are defined as

\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}:=\left\{V:\ \|V\|_{L^{q_{1},q_{2}}_{x,t}}<\infty\ \text{ where }\ \frac{d}{q_{1}}+\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{q_{2}}=1+q_{m,d}\right\},

(1.3)

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}:=\left\{V:\ \|\nabla V\|_{L^{\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2}}_{x,t}}<\infty\ \text{ where }\ \frac{d}{\tilde{q}_{1}}+\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{\tilde{q}_{2}}=2+q_{m,d}\right\}.

(1.4)

Moreover, let us name
\|V\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}}
and
\|V\|_{\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}}
, as the scaling invariant norms corresponding to each spaces.

(ii)

The sub-scaling classes are defined as

\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}:=\left\{V:\ \|V\|_{L^{q_{1},q_{2}}_{x,t}}<\infty\ \text{ where }\ \frac{d}{q_{1}}+\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{q_{2}}\leq 1+q_{m,d}\right\},

(1.5)

\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}:=\left\{V:\ \|\nabla V\|_{L^{\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2}}_{x,t}}<\infty\ \text{ where }\ \frac{d}{\tilde{q}_{1}}+\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{\tilde{q}_{2}}\leq 2+q_{m,d}\right\}.

(1.6)

Let us name
\|V\|_{\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}}
and
\|V\|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}}
, as the sub-scaling norms corresponding to each spaces.

We remark that the main existence results are given under certain assumptions on
V
in sub-scaling classes. The critical case matters the most and the strict-subcritical case (the case of strict inequality in
(
1.5
) or (
1.6
))
is simpler, and therefore, proofs throughout the paper concern only the case of scaling invariant classes.

Remark 1.2

(i)

In Figure 1, we graph the lines of
(\frac{1}{q_{1}},\frac{1}{q_{2}})
satisfying
\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}
defined in (
1.3
) for
m,q\geq 1
. We easily observe that
q_{m,d}
becomes
0
as
m\to 1
or
q\to\infty
, which corresponds that
\frac{d}{q_{1}}+\frac{2}{q_{2}}=1
as in (
1.2
).
The condition (
1.2
) appears in the context of understanding heat equations
(m=1)
or
L^{\infty}
-sense scaling invariant classes of porous medium equations

(m>1,q=\infty)
(see e.g.
[
17
,
39
,
33
]
). Therefore, our scaling invariant condition (
1.3
) is more comprehensive including the linear case, i.e.
m=1
or the case of PME for existence of
L^{\infty}
-solutions.

(ii)

In Figure 2, we graph lines of
(\frac{1}{\tilde{q}_{1}},\frac{1}{\tilde{q}_{2}})
satisfying
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}

defined in (
1.4
) for
m,q\geq 1
. In fact, by using embedding property, one can supress the norm of
V
by the norm of
\nabla V
; that is,

\|V\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}}:=\|V\|_{L^{q_{1},q_{2}}_{x,t}}\lesssim\|V\|_{\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}}:=\|\nabla V\|_{L^{\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2}}_{x,t}}

(1.7)

provided

\tilde{q}_{1}=\frac{dq_{1}}{d+q_{1}}\in(1,d)\ (\text{equivalently }q_{1}=\frac{d\tilde{q}_{1}}{d-\tilde{q}_{1}}\in(\frac{d}{d-1},\infty)),
and

\tilde{q}_{2}=q_{2}
. Hence, the range of
\tilde{q}_{2}
is

\begin{cases}\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+{q_{m,d}}}<\tilde{q}_{2}\leq\infty,&\text{ if }1<m<1+\frac{q(d-2)}{d}\\
\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+{q_{m,d}}}<\tilde{q}_{2}<\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{2-d+q_{m,d}},&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.8)

In Figure 2, the intersection of lines and shaded region is where (
1.8
) holds.

(iii)

Let
1<q<q^{L}
and let
(q_{1}^{L},q_{2}^{L})
be a pair satisfying
\mathcal{S}_{m,q^{L}}^{(q_{1}^{L},q_{2}^{L})}
. Concerning
\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1}^{L},q_{2})}
and
\mathcal{S}_{m,q^{L}}^{(q_{1}^{L},q_{2}^{L})}
, we observe that
q_{2}^{L}>q_{2}
illustrated in Figure 1. Moreover, the Hölder inequality in
t\in[0,T]
yields

\|V\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1}^{L},q_{2})}}\leq T^{\frac{1}{q_{2}}-\frac{1}{q_{2}^{L}}}\|V\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m,q^{L}}^{(q_{1}^{L},q_{2}^{L})}}.

(1.9)

Our main objective is to establish the existence of
L^{q}
-weak solutions of (
1.1
) subject to
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
1\leq q<\infty
.
More specifically, we provide sufficient conditions of drift term
V
or
\nabla V
to ensure the existence of solutions for (
1.1
) with
L^{q}
-initial data (the collection of
V
or
\nabla V
is defined in (
1.5
) or (
1.6
)).
An interesting feature is that such conditions of
V
or
\nabla V
depend on
m
and
q
, which is one of major differences compared to linear equation with drift term, since (
1.2
) of the linear case is independent of
q
(compare to
[
36
]
).

As far as authors understand, weak solutions of PME with general drift terms have been barely studied, although weak solutions of homogeneous PME or coupled system with PME type with gradient flow structures have been extensively studied so far. Since the PME with drift terms under our considerations, in general, does not have either
maximum principle or gradient flow structure, existence of weak solutions does not seem to be established by the existing methods of proofs based on such arguments.
In fact, our main tool in constructing weak solutions is, so called splitting method, which solves the PME with drift term by solving homogeneous PME and transport equations
via flow maps separately, which enables us to obtain aprroximated solutions and convergence to weak solutions. We are positive that there are probably other ways of proving existence of weak soltuions, but they must be independent of maximum principle and gradient flow structure, and thus we think that our proof is a theoretical way of new approach. We expect that our method of the proof and consequential results could be applicable to broader classes of equations.

We make several comments, for simplicity, we deal with only the case
d\geq 3
in the tables below (two dimensional case is also treated in similar ways but there are little differences such as range of parameters’ values, compared to higher dimensions).

Firstly, we distinguish the case
q=1
and
q>1
. For the case
q=1
(in Table
1
), in general, we need to restrict
m\in(1,2]
, since a priori entropy estimate is not clear for the case
m>2
, unless
V
is divergence-free or
\nabla V
belongs sub-scaling classes. Compactness arguments also give the same restriction on
m
, even for the cases of
\nabla\cdot V=0
or
V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,1}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}
.

In addition, initial data requires more than just
L^{1}
, and more precisely it is necessary that
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho_{0}\log\rho_{0}dx<\infty
due to entropy estimates. In this case, we suppose that
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,1}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}
with
2\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{m}{m-1}
. Furthermore, if
\rho_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
with
1<p\leq{\lambda_{1}}:=1+\frac{1}{d(m-1)+1}
, we can obtain the
p
-th moment estimates and the
speed
(see Remark
3.12
) estimates, which assure the existence of weak solutions in Wasserstein spaces
(see Theorem
2.3
).

Similar analysis can be performed for the case

V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,1}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}
(refer Theorem
2.5
) or
V
is divergence-free (see Theorem
2.4
).
In divergence-free case, we emphasize that the range of subscaling class becomes wider, i.e.
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,1}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}
with
{\lambda_{1}}\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{{\lambda_{1}}m}{m-1}
, mainly because the drift term does not show up in the energy estimate via integration by parts. We refer Section
6
for the details of proofs.

On the other hand (see Table 2), in case
q>1
, when usual
L^{q}
-energy estimates are performed, it is necessary to assmue that
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
Here we need to consider
1<m\leq 2
and
m>2
separately.
In case that
m>2
, an additional restriction,
q\geq m-1
is necessary for the existence of weak solutions (see Theorem
2.7
).
Indeed, when
L^{q}
-energy estimates and compactness arguments are conducted, we should control the quantity,
\rho
to the power
q-m+1
, which requres nonnegative sign that is equivalent to
q\geq m-1
.
This restriction might be technical but we do not know how to handle
the case of
1<q<m-1
, and thus we leave such case as an open question.

In case
m>2
, the restriction on
q\geq m-1
can be relaxed to
q>\frac{m}{2}
if either
V
is divergence-free or
\nabla V
is under consideration (refer Theorem
2.11
and
2.15
). This is because the restriction
q>\frac{m}{2}
for
m>2
is required for compactness arguments while a priori
L^{q}
-energy estimates are obtained for all
m,q>1
.

The same restrictions on
m
and
q
are required, when we look for weak solutions in Wasserstein space (see Table 3). In such case, the subscaling class of
V
or
\nabla V
becomes narrower because
the
p
-th moment and the speed estimates are shown to be valid for more restrictive values of parameters
(see Theorem
2.9

(i)
, Theorem
2.13

(i)
and Theorem
2.16

(i)
for the details).

Therefore, it could happen that
there is a weak solution satisfying only energy estimates but we do not know if it belongs to Wasserstein spaces, because subscaling class of
V
corresponding to energy space turns out to be wider than that of
V
relevant to Wasserstein spaces.
Nevertheless, using temporal embedding, some cases guarantee that weak solutions with energy estimates belong to Wasserstein spaces as well (see Theorem
2.9

(ii)
, Theorem
2.13

(ii)
and Theorem
2.16

(ii)
for the details).

As a main tool, we adopt the splitting method to establish approximated solutions, which converges to a
L^{q}
-weak solution (see section
5
and section
6
). In the process of constructing the approximated solutions, we use that Hölder continuity of PME in the absense of drift term is preserved uniformly up to initial time, which seems to be of independt interest (see Appendix
A
).

On the other hand, uniqueness for solutions of (
1.1
) does not seem to be obvious, since the maximum principle, in general, is not true due to nonlinear diffusion with drift term. Nevertheless, we obtain uniqueness result of
L^{m}
-weak solutions under additional conditions for gradient of drift and initial data, exploiting mass transportation theory (see Theorem
2.18
and Corollary
2.19
, and refer Section
7
for proofs).

As an application, we consider a repulsive Keller-Segel system of porous medium type.
With the aid of existence results, if the power
m
of degenerate diffusion is beyond a certain positive number, we show that
solutions become bounded, which turns out to be an improvement, compared to known results (see Theorem
2.21
and refer Section
7
for its proof). Existence results developed in this paper are expected to be applicable to other situations related to equations of PME type as well.

Our paper is organized as follows:
In Section
2
, we state all main results and several remarks and figures are provided to help readers understand.
Some preliminaries are prepared in Section
3
. Section
4
is devoted to giving a priori estimates for regular solutions (see Definition
4.1
).
In Section
5
, the existence of regular solutions of PME is established by the splitting method.
Section
6
is prepared for proofs of existence results stated in Section
2
.
Finally, we provide the proof of uniqueness results and we treat a repulsive Keller-Segel system of porous medium type in Section
7
.
We present the proof of uniform Hölder continuity of homogeneous PME up to initial time in Appendix
A
.
In Appendix
B
, supplemetary figures are additionally added.

2
Main results

In this section, we describe our main results and make relevant remarks for each of them. First, existence of weak solutions for (
1.1
) are categorized according to hypotheses of given initial data and drifts.
Uniqueness of weak solutions are discussed as well. Finally, as an application, we study a repulsive type Keller-Segel equations to improve previously known results by taking advantage of develped main results.

Before stating main theorems, for convenience, we introduce some notations and make a few remarks.

•

Let us denote by
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the set of all Borel
probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Furthermore, denote
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with a finite
p
-th moment, that is
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle x\rangle^{p}\,d\mu<\infty
if

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We refer Section
3.2
for definitions and related properties of the Wasserstein distance denoted by
W_{p}
, the Wasserstein space, and AC (absolutely continuous) curves.

•

The
p
-th power is given as

\langle x\rangle^{p}=\left(1+|x|^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}},\ \quad\text{ for }\ 1\leq p<\infty\ \text{ and }\ x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\ d\geq 2.

•

Let
A\subset\mathbb{R}
be a measurable set. We denote by
\delta_{A}
the indicator function of
A
.

•

For any
m>1
and
q\geq 1
, let us define the following constant

\lambda_{q}:=\min\left\{2,1+\frac{d(q-1)+q}{d(m-1)+q}\right\},

(2.1)

that can be rewritten as
{\lambda_{q}}=\left\{1+\frac{d(q-1)+q}{d(m-1)+q}\right\}\cdot\delta_{\{1\leq q\leq m\}}+2\cdot\delta_{\{q>m\}}
.
The constant
{\lambda_{q}}\in(1,2]
in (
2.1
) naturally comes from evaluating the
p
-th moment and the speed estimates which are essential to play in Wasserstein spaces (refer Section
4.1
). A straightforward observation gives that
1+\frac{d(q-1)+q}{d(m-1)+q}=2
if
q=m
.
Also, for any
q\geq 1
, note that
{\lambda_{q}}=2
if
m=1
.

•

The letters
c
and
C
are used for generic constants. Also, the letter
\theta
is a generic constant which varies with arguments of interpolation.
Throughout the paper, we omit the dependence on
m
,
q
,
d
,
T
, and
\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}
, because we regard
m>1
,
q\geq 1
,
d\geq 2
,
T>0
are given constants and
\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}=1

in either
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
or
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

•

In figures, the notation
\cal{R}(\textbf{Ab\ldots Yz})
is used to indicate a polygon with vertices
\textbf{A},\textbf{b},\ldots,\textbf{Y},\textbf{z}
. Also the notation
\overline{\textbf{Ab\ldots Yz}}
is used for the piecewise line segments connecting
A
,
b
,
\ldots
,
Y
,
z
.

Here we introduce the notion of weak solutions of (
1.1
).

Definition 2.1

Let
q\in[1,\infty)
and
V
be a measurable vector field.
We say that a nonnegative measurable function
\rho
is
a
L^{q}
-weak solution
of (
1.1
) with
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if the followings are satisfied:

(i)

It holds that

\rho\in L^{\infty}\left(0,T;\left(L^{1}\cap L^{q}\right)(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)\cap L^{m}(Q_{T}),\quad\nabla\rho^{\frac{m+q-1}{2}}\in L^{2}(Q_{T}),\quad\text{and}\quad\rho V\in L^{1}(Q_{T}).

(ii)

For any function
\varphi\in{\mathcal{C}}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[0,T))
it holds that

\iint_{Q_{T}}\left\{\rho\varphi_{t}+\rho^{m}\Delta\varphi+\rho V\cdot\nabla\varphi\right\}\,dxdt=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{0}(\cdot)\varphi(\cdot,0)\,dx.

(2.2)

We remind the property of
mass conservation
for nonnegative solutions of (
1.1
), i.e.

\|\rho(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. This can be derived from the weak formulation (see e.g.
[
52
, Section 9.5.1]
).

Remark 2.2

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is,
\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}=1
. We note that the mass conservation property in time implies that
\|\rho(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1
a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.

If
\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}=c>0
, then we replace
\rho
by
\tilde{\rho}=\rho/c
. Thus we have
\tilde{\rho}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and

\partial_{t}\rho+\Delta\rho^{m}=\nabla\cdot(V\rho)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\partial_{t}\tilde{\rho}+c^{m-1}\Delta(\tilde{\rho})^{m}=\nabla\cdot(V\tilde{\rho}).

In fact, the diffusive coefficient
c^{m-1}
of the equation for
\tilde{\rho}
does not play important role in our analysis, and thus, for simplicity, we assume that
c=1
.

2.1
Existence results

In this subsection, we state all existence results and related remarks and figures are presented.

2.1.1
Existence for case:
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho_{0}\log\rho_{0}\,dx<\infty
.

In this subsection, we assume that the initial data satisfy

\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho_{0}\log\rho_{0}\,dx<\infty,

(2.3)

where
p\in(1,2]
will be specified later.
We remark that it is not enough to assume merely
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for showing the existence of
L^{1}
-weak solutions. Instead, with slightly better initial data satisfying (
2.3
), we compute a priori estimates in Proposition
4.5
and Proposition
4.9
, which enable us to obtain
existence of weak solutions via suitable approximations.
Here we consider separately three different cases depending on the condition of
V
.
Firstly, Theorem
2.3
is when
V
belongs to a certain subscaling classses.
Moreover, Theorem
2.4
deals with the case that
V
is a divergence-free vector and Theorem
2.5
is for the case
\nabla V
belongs a sub-scaling classes, respectively.
The restriction of
1<m\leq 2
appears in general because of compactness arguments (see Remark
2.6

(ii)
).

For the first case, our result reads as follows:

Theorem 2.3

Let
1<m\leq 2
and
p>1
. Suppose that
(
2.3
) and

V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,1}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\quad\text{for}\quad\begin{cases}2\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{m}{m-1},&\text{ if }d>2,\\
2\leq q_{2}<\frac{m}{m-1},&\text{ if }d=2.\end{cases}

(2.4)

(i)

For
1<p\leq{\lambda_{1}}:=1+\frac{1}{d(m-1)+1}
, then there exists
a nonnegative
L^{1}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) in Definition
2.1
such that
\rho\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
\rho(\cdot,0)=\rho_{0}
.
Furthermore,
\rho
satisfies

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{t\}}\rho\left(\left|\log\rho\right|+\langle x\rangle^{p}\right)\,dx+\iint_{Q_{T}}\{\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right|^{2}+\left(\left|\frac{\nabla\rho^{m}}{\rho}\right|^{p}+|V|^{p}\right)\rho\}\,dx\,dt\leq C,

(2.5)

and

W_{p}(\rho(t),\rho(s))\leq C(t-s)^{\frac{p-1}{p}},\qquad\forall~~0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

(2.6)

where the constant
C=C(\|V\|_{\mathfrak{S}_{m,1}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}},\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho_{0}\log\rho_{0}+\rho_{0}\langle x\rangle^{p}\right)\,dx)
.

(ii)

For
p>{\lambda_{1}}
, the same conclusions hold as in
(i)
except that
p
is replaced by
{\lambda_{1}}
.
That is, there exists
a nonnegative
L^{1}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) in Definition
2.1
such that
\rho\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{{\lambda_{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
\rho(\cdot,0)=\rho_{0}
.
Furthermore,
\rho
satisfies

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{t\}}\rho\left(\left|\log\rho\right|+\langle x\rangle^{{\lambda_{1}}}\right)\,dx+\iint_{Q_{T}}\{\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right|^{2}+\left(\left|\frac{\nabla\rho^{m}}{\rho}\right|^{{\lambda_{1}}}+|V|^{{\lambda_{1}}}\right)\rho\}\,dx\,dt\leq C,

(2.7)

and

W_{{\lambda_{1}}}(\rho(t),\rho(s))\leq C(t-s)^{\frac{{\lambda_{1}}-1}{{\lambda_{1}}}},\qquad\forall~~0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

(2.8)

where the constant
C=C(\|V\|_{\mathfrak{S}_{m,1}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}},\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho_{0}\log\rho_{0}+\rho_{0}\langle x\rangle^{p}\right)\,dx)
.

In the next theorem,
V
is assumed to be divergence-free.
The scaling invariant class of
V
is larger than that of Theorem
2.3
(See Remark
2.6

(iii)
and Fig. 3 for a more definte comparison).

Theorem 2.4

Let
1<m\leq 2
and
p>1
. Assume (
2.3
) and the divergence-free
V
(i.e.,
\nabla\cdot V=0
) satisfying

V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,1}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\quad\text{for}\quad\begin{cases}{\lambda_{1}}\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{{\lambda_{1}}m}{m-1},&\text{ if }d>2,\\
{\lambda_{1}}\leq q_{2}<\frac{{\lambda_{1}}m}{m-1},&\text{ if }d=2,\end{cases}

(2.9)

where
{\lambda_{1}}
is the number defined in Theorem
2.3
.
Then the same conclusions hold as in Theorem
2.3
.

The last theorem is concerned about the case that
\nabla V
belongs to a sub-scaling class (see Remark
2.6

(iv)
).

Theorem 2.5

Let
1<m\leq 2
and let
p>1
. Suppose that (
2.3
) and

V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,1}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}\frac{2+d(m-1)}{1+d(m-1)}<\tilde{q}_{2}\leq\frac{m}{m-1},&\text{ if }d>2,\\
\frac{2+d(m-1)}{1+d(m-1)}<\tilde{q}_{2}<\frac{m}{m-1},&\text{ if
}d=2.\end{cases}

(2.10)

Then the same conclusions hold as in Theorem
2.3

with

C=C(\|V\|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,1}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}},\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho_{0}\log\rho_{0}+\rho_{0}\langle x\rangle^{p}\right)\,dx)
.

Let us provide some remarks regarding three theorems in this subsection.

Remark 2.6

(i)

Results in Theorem
2.3
are also true for linear case
(m=1)
for
1\leq p\leq 2
(see
[
36
]
). We do not know whether or not the moment estimate (
2.5
) can be extended to
p>{\lambda_{1}}
, and thus we leave it as an open question.

(ii)

There is restriction on
m
that
1<m\leq 2
in Theorem
2.4
and
2.5
for cases
\nabla\cdot V=0
or
V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,1}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}
, though a priori estimates are obtained for
m>1
in Proposition
4.5
(ii), (iii). Such a restriction is necessary because the compactness argument in Proposition
4.14
only works for
1<m\leq 2
.
Therefore, if one can resolve the compactness argument in cases
\nabla\cdot V=0
or
V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,1}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}
for
m>2
, then the results in Theorem
2.4
and
2.5
become valid for any
m>1
.

(iii)

In the following figure, we illustrate the range of
(q_{1},q_{2})
satisfying (
2.4
) and (
2.9
) in particular for scaling invariant class
\mathcal{S}_{m,1}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}
as dark-shaded region
\mathcal{R}\left(\textbf{ABC}\right)
and as lightly-shaded region
\mathcal{R}\left(\textbf{ABDE}\right)
, respectively. When
d=2
, the line
\overline{\textbf{BC}}
and
\overline{\textbf{BD}}
are excluded from
\mathcal{R}\left(\textbf{ABC}\right)
and
\mathcal{R}\left(\textbf{ABDE}\right)
, respectively.

) in

in Section

(including
D

E
if
d>2
and excluding both if
d=2
).

2.1.2
Existence for case:
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ q>1

We first state results regarding the existence of a
L^{q}
-weak solution satisfying an energy inequality.
In case that
1<m\leq 2
, there is no restriction on
q
that
q>1
.
It is, however, not clear, for the case
m>2
and, in fact, according to our analysis, it is requred that
q\geq m-1
. We also construct a
L^{q}
-weak solution satisfying moments and speed estimates as well as an energy inequality, and the restriction
q\geq m-1
is necessary as similarly, if
m>2
(Theorem
2.9
).

Now we start with
L^{q}
-weak solutions satisfying energy inequality.

Theorem 2.7

Let
m>1
. Also let
q>1
and
q\geq m-1
.
Assume that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and

V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}2\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{q+m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }d>2\\
2\leq q_{2}<\frac{q+m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }d=2.\end{cases}

(2.11)

Then, there exists a nonnegative
L^{q}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) in Definition
2.1

that holds

\esssup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{q}(\cdot,t)\,dx+\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{2}\,dx\,dt\leq C,

(2.12)

with
C=C(\|V\|_{\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}},\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})})
.

We make a few remarks regarding Theorem
2.7
.

Remark 2.8

(i)

Figure 4 (for cases:
1<m\leq 2
,
q>1
) is to visualize the pairs of
(q_{1},q_{2})
satisfying (
2.11
) in
\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}
for
d>2
as the shaded region
\mathcal{R}(\textbf{ABCD})
. If
d=2
, then the line
\overline{\textbf{BC}}
does not included to the shaded region. The points
\textbf{e}\to\textbf{A}
and
\textbf{f}\to\textbf{B}
in both cases either
m\to 1
or
q\to\infty
. Moreover, we observe that
\textbf{C}\to\textbf{B}
and
\textbf{D}\to\textbf{A}
as
m\to 1
. Also, when
m=2
, then
\textbf{C}=\textbf{D}=(1/2,1/2)
.

(ii)

Figure 5 (for cases:
m>2
,

) is illustrating the pairs of
(q_{1},q_{2})
satisfying (

) in

as the shaded region
\mathcal{R}(\textbf{ABC})
for

. If

, then the line
\overline{\textbf{BC}}
does not included to the shaded region. The points
\textbf{e}\to\textbf{A}
and
\textbf{f}\to\textbf{B}
as
q\to\infty
.

In the next theorem, we express the existence of a
L^{q}
-weak solution as an absolutely continuous curve in Wasserstein space for
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. More precisely,
p
-th moment and speed estimates are additionally added, compared to weak solutions in Theorem
2.7
.
Due to constraint of handling speed and
p
-th moment estimates for
p\leq 2
, the condition on
V
is subject to restrain when
q>m
that (
2.13
) gives smaller region compared to (
2.11
) (cf. Figure 4 Vs. 4-
(i)
, Figure 5 Vs. 5-
(i)
). We refer Section
4.3
for more details.
Meanwhile, because the temporal domain is bounded, we are able to extend conditions on
V
(meaningful when
q>m
) by applying embedding arguments in Proposition
4.11
.

Theorem 2.9

Let
m>1
and
p>1
. Also let
q>1
and
q\geq m-1
.
Suppose that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

(i)

Assume that

V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}q_{1}\leq\frac{2m}{m-1},\ 2\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{q+m-1}{m-1}\,\delta_{\{1<q\leq m\}}+\frac{2m-1}{m-1}\,\delta_{\{q>m\}},&\text{ if }d>2,\\
q_{1}\leq\frac{2m}{m-1},\ 2\leq q_{2}<\frac{q+m-1}{m-1}\,\delta_{\{1<q\leq m\}}+\frac{2m-1}{m-1}\,\delta_{\{q>m\}},&\text{ if }d=2.\end{cases}

(2.13)

(a)

Then, for
1<p\leq{\lambda_{q}}:=\min\left\{2,1+\frac{d(q-1)+q}{d(m-1)+q}\right\}
, there exists a nonnegative
L^{q}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) in Definition
2.1
such that
\rho\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
\rho(\cdot,0)=\rho_{0}
.
In addition,
\rho
satisfies (
2.6
) and

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho^{q}+\rho\langle x\rangle^{p}\right)\,dx+\iint_{Q_{T}}\{\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{2}+\left(\left|\frac{\nabla\rho^{m}}{\rho}\right|^{p}+|V|^{p}\right)\rho\}\,dx\,dt\leq C,

(2.14)

where
C=C(\|V\|_{\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}},\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho_{0}^{q}+\rho_{0}\langle x\rangle^{p}\right)\,dx)
.

(b)

For
p>{\lambda_{q}}
, the same conclusions hold as in (a) except that
p
is replaced by
{\lambda_{q}}
.

(ii)

(Embedding) Let
q>m
. Assume that

V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}q_{1}\leq\frac{2m}{m-1},\ \frac{2m-1}{m-1}<q_{2}\leq\frac{q+m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }d>2,\\
q_{1}\leq\frac{2m}{m-1},\ \frac{2m-1}{m-1}\leq q_{2}<\frac{q+m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }d=2.\end{cases}

(2.15)

For
(q_{1},q_{2})
in (
2.15
), there exists a constant

q_{2}^{\ast}=q_{2}^{\ast}(q_{1})\in[2,q_{2})
such that
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,q^{\ast}}^{(q_{1},q_{2}^{\ast})}
satisfying
(
2.13
).
Furthermore, the same conclusions hold as in
(i)
except that
{\lambda_{q}}
is replaced by
{\lambda_{q^{\ast}}}
.

Here are a few remarks about Theorem
2.9
.

Remark 2.10

(i)

Results in Theorem
2.9
are also true for the heat equation (
m=1
) with drift term for
1<p\leq 2
(see
[
36
]
). We do not know whether or not the moment estimate (
2.14
) can be extended to
p>{\lambda_{q}}
, and thus we leave those as open questions.

(ii)

When
1<q\leq m
, the condition (
2.13
) is the same as (
2.11
). When
q>m
, we use speed and
p
-th moment estimates for
q=m
, therefore the condition (
4.2
) for
q=m
is the same as (
2.13
) for
q>m
(refer Proposition
4.10

(i)
).

2.1.3
Existence for case:
\nabla\cdot V=0
and
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ q>1
.

Here we assume that
V
is a divergence-free vector field. Compared to Section
2.1.2
, theorems in this subsection work for less restricted range for
q
and less restrictions on
V
in sub-scaling class.

Let us define

q_{2}^{M}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\left(\frac{d(q+m-1)+q}{d(q+m-1)+2q}-\frac{q}{q+m-1}\right)^{-1},&\text{if }1<q<q^{\ast},\\
\infty,&\text{if }q\geq q^{\ast},\end{array}\right.

where

q^{\ast}:=\frac{2d(m-1)}{\sqrt{d^{2}+6d+1}-(d+1)}.

(2.16)

In this case, the existence result is stated for
L^{q}
-weak solutions with an energy inequality as follows:

Theorem 2.11

Let
m>1
. Also let
q>\max\{1,\frac{m}{2}\}
. Suppose that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
Furthermore, assume that
V
is divergence-free (that is,
\nabla\cdot V=0
) and

V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\quad\text{for}\quad\begin{cases}\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+q_{m,d}}\leq q_{2}\leq q_{2}^{M},&\text{if }d>2,\\
\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+q_{m,d}}\leq q_{2}<q_{2}^{M}\cdot\delta_{\{1<q\leq q^{\ast}\}},&\text{if }d=2,\\
\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+q_{m,d}}\leq q_{2}\leq q_{2}^{M}\cdot\delta_{\{q>q^{\ast}\}},&\text{if }d=2.\end{cases}

(2.17)

Then, there exists
a nonnegative
L^{q}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) in Definition
2.1
that holds

\displaystyle\esssup_{0\leq t\leq T}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{q}(\cdot,t)dx+\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{2}\,dx\,dt\leq C,

(2.18)

where
C=C(\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})})
.

We make a few remarks regarding the above theorem.

Remark 2.12

(i)

The restriction on (
2.17
) is directly from Lemma
4.17
and Proposition
4.19

(c)
for carrying compactness arguments which is essential to show the convergence of approximated solutions, although a priori estimate is calculated for any
m,q>1
in Proposition
4.6
(iii). Moreover, we note that (
2.17
) is weaker than (
2.11
) or (
2.19
).

(ii)

The range of
q>\max\{1,\frac{m}{2}\}
is sorted into two cases: either
1<m\leq 2
and
q>1
, or
m>2
and
q>\frac{m}{2}
.
However, the compactness arguments in Proposition
4.19

(ii)
works for
q>\frac{m}{2}
(which is strictly less than
m-1
for
m>2
). In fact, when
m>2
, the range
q\geq m-1
in Proposition
4.19

(i)
is weakened to
q>\frac{m}{2}
thanks to
\nabla\cdot V=0
.

(iii)

Here we plot the region of pairs
(q_{1},q_{2})
satisfying (
2.17
) in
\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}
for

,
q>1
,
d>2
.
Note that
\textbf{E},\frac{1+q_{m,d}}{2+q_{m,d}},\textbf{f}\to\textbf{A}
and
\textbf{C},\frac{1+q_{m,d}}{d}\to\textbf{B}
as either
m\to 1
or
q\to\infty
. When
d=2
, the line
\overline{\textbf{CD}}
is excluded from the region
\mathcal{R}(\textbf{ABCDE})
.
There is Figure 6-
(ii)
for
m>2
and

in Appendix

.

is on

on

Next, we show the existence of a
L^{q}
-weak solution in Wasserstein space in which speed and moment estimates play importantly. The second part of the following theorem is obtained by embedding in time variable. Unlike result in Theorem
2.9

(ii)
, one can make observation that the embedding argument is meaningful for all
q>1
.

Theorem 2.13

Let
m>1
and
p>1
. Also let
q>1
and
q\geq\frac{m}{2}
. Suppose that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
\nabla\cdot V=0
.

(i)

Suppose that

V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}{\lambda_{q}}\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{{\lambda_{q}}(q+m-1)}{q+m-2},&\text{ if }d>2,\ 1<q\leq m,\\
q_{1}\leq\frac{2m}{m-1},\ 2\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{2m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }d>2,\ q>m,\\
{\lambda_{q}}\leq q_{2}<\frac{{\lambda_{q}}(q+m-1)}{q+m-2},&\text{ if }d=2,\ 1<q\leq m,\\
q_{1}\leq\frac{2m}{m-1},\ 2\leq q_{2}<\frac{2m-1}{m-1},&\text{
if }d=2,\ q>m.\end{cases}

(2.19)

(a)

Then, for
1<p\leq{\lambda_{q}}:=\min\left\{2,1+\frac{d(q-1)+q}{d(m-1)+q}\right\}
, there exists a nonnegative
L^{q}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) in Definition
2.1
such that

\rho\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
\rho(\cdot,0)=\rho_{0}
.
Furthermore,
\rho

satisfies (
2.18
), (
2.14
), and (
2.6
) with

C=C(\left\|V\right\|_{\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}},\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho_{0}^{q}+\rho_{0}\langle x\rangle^{p}\right)\,dx)
.

(b)

For
p>{\lambda_{q}}
, the same conclusions hold as in (a) except that
p
is replace by
{\lambda_{q}}
.

(ii)

(Embedding) Suppose that

V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+q_{m,d}}<q_{2}<\frac{{\lambda_{1}}m}{m-1},&\text{ if }1<q\leq\frac{md}{d-1},\\
\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+q_{m,d}}<q_{2}\leq\infty,&\text{ if }q>\frac{md}{d-1}.\end{cases}

(2.20)

For given
(q_{1},q_{2})
in (
2.20
), there
exists constants
q_{2}^{\ast}=q_{2}^{\ast}(q_{1})\in[2,q_{2}]

such that
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{m,q^{\ast}}^{(q_{1},q_{2}^{\ast})}

satisfying (
2.19
).
Furthermore, the same conclusion
holds as in
(i)
except that
{\lambda_{q}}
is replaced by
{\lambda_{q^{\ast}}}
.

Now we explain more on the condition (
2.19
) in Theorem
2.13
.

and

if

.

.

2.1.4
Existence for case:
\nabla V
in sub-scaling classes

Now we assume that
\nabla V
is satisfying sub-scaling classes, that is,
V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}
.
We then see that there are corresponding existence results and collect them in this subsection when
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The first result is concerned about
L^{q}
-weak solutions with an energy inequality.

Theorem 2.15

Let
m>1
. Also let
q>\max\{1,\frac{m}{2}\}
. Suppose that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and

V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+q_{m,d}}<\tilde{q}_{2}\leq\frac{q+m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }d>2,\\
\ \frac{2+q_{m,2}}{1+q_{m,2}}<\tilde{q}_{2}<\frac{q+m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }d=2.\end{cases}

(2.21)

Then, there exists a nonnegative
L^{q}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) in Definition
2.1

satisfying (
2.12
) with

C=C(\left\|V\right\|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}},\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})
.

The second result is the existence of a
L^{q}
-weak solution as an absolutely continuous curve in Wasserstein space.

Theorem 2.16

Let
m>1
and
p>1
. Also let
q>1
and
q\geq\frac{m}{2}
. Suppose that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

(i)

Assume that

V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{1+q_{m,d}}<\tilde{q}_{2}\leq\frac{q+m-1}{m-1}\,\delta_{\{1<q\leq m\}}+\frac{2m-1}{m-1}\,\delta_{\{q>m\}},&\text{ if }d>2,\\
\frac{2+q_{m,2}}{1+q_{m,2}}<\tilde{q}_{2}<\frac{q+m-1}{m-1}\,\delta_{\{1<q\leq m\}}+\frac{2m-1}{m-1}\,\delta_{\{q>m\}},&\text{ if }d=2.\end{cases}

(2.22)

(a)

Then, for
1<p\leq{\lambda_{q}}:=\min\left\{2,1+\frac{d(q-1)+q}{d(m-1)+q}\right\}
,
there exists a nonnegative
L^{q}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) in Definition
2.1
such that
\rho\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
\rho(\cdot,0)=\rho_{0}
.
Furthermore,
\rho
satisfies (
2.12
) with
C=C(\left\|V\right\|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}},\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})})
. Also,
(
2.14
) and (
2.6
) hold with
C=C(\left\|V\right\|_{\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}},\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho_{0}^{q}+\rho_{0}\langle x\rangle^{p}\right)\,dx)
.

(b)

For
p>{\lambda_{q}}
, the same conclusions hold as in (a) except that
p
is replaced by
{\lambda_{q}}
.

(ii)

(Embedding) Let
q>m
.
Assume that

V\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}\frac{2m-1}{m-1}<\tilde{q}_{2}\leq\frac{q+m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }d>2,\\
\frac{2m-1}{m-1}\leq\tilde{q}_{2}<\frac{q+m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if
}d=2.\end{cases}

(2.23)

For given
(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})
in
(
2.23
), there exists a constant

\tilde{q}_{2}^{\ast}=\tilde{q}_{2}^{\ast}(\tilde{q}_{1})\in(\frac{2+q^{\ast}_{m,d}}{1+q^{\ast}_{m,d}},\tilde{q}_{2}]
such that

V\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{m,q^{\ast}}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2}^{\ast})}
satisfying (
2.22
).
Furthermore,
the same conclusion holds as in Theorem
2.16

(i)
except
that
{\lambda_{q}}
is replaced by
{\lambda_{q^{\ast}}}
.

Here we make a few remarks of Theorem
2.15
and Theorem
2.16
.

Remark 2.17

(i)

When
V\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}
, a priori estimate is calculated for any
m,q>1
in Proposition
4.6
(ii), but the restriction
q>\frac{m}{2}
is required because of the compactness arguments in Proposition
4.19

(ii)
. In Theorem
2.15
, we allow the limiting case
q=\frac{m}{2}
if
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
with
C

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho_{0}\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx

V

2.21
).

2.2
Uniqueness

The uniqueness of
L^{q}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) is not obvious because, in general, the maximal principle fails for nonlinear diffusive equations with drifts. However, with additional assumptions on
\nabla V
and restriction on
q
, we are able to obtain uniqueness results.
Here, we state the uniqueness of
L^{m}
-weak solution of (
1.1
).

Theorem 2.18

Let
\rho_{0}\in L^{m}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Assume
V
satisfy

V\in\mathcal{S}_{m,m}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}2\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{2m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }\ d>2,\\
2\leq q_{2}<\frac{2m-1}{m-1},&\text{ if }\ d=2.\end{cases}

(2.24)

Assume further
\nabla V\in L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}(Q_{T})
. Suppose
that
\rho
is a
L^{m}
-weak solution of (
1.1
) such that

\rho:[0,T]\mapsto\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is narrowly
continuous. Then it is unique.

Next corollary is an immediate consequence of Theorem
2.18
, which reads as follows:

Corollary 2.19

Assume
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
p\geq 2
and
q\geq m
.
Let
V
satisfy the hypothesis in one of Theorems
2.9
,
2.13
and
2.16
, and let
\nabla V\in L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}(Q_{T})
.
Suppose that
\rho
is narrowly continuous
L^{m}
-weak solution of (
1.1
). Then it is unique.

Remark 2.20

(i)

The notion of narrowly continuous is the continuity in the weak* topology of
\left(\mathcal{C}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\right)^{\prime}
(refer (
3.7
) in Section
3.2
).

(ii)

Assume
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
p\geq 2
and
q\geq m
.
In Theorems
2.9
,
2.13
and
2.16
, for each
1<\tilde{p}\leq p
, we show the existence of
L^{q}
-weak solution
\rho_{\tilde{p}}
satisfying

\rho_{\tilde{p}}\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{\tilde{p}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
. Especially, this implies that
\rho_{\tilde{p}}:[0,T]\mapsto\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is narrowly continuous.
We note that
\rho_{\tilde{p}}
is also
L^{m}
-weak solution.
Hence, if we further assume

\nabla V\in L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}(Q_{T})
, then all these solutions
\rho_{\tilde{p}}
are identical.

(iii)

We do not know the uniqueness of
L^{m}
-weak solutions in the absence of the hypothesis
\nabla V\in L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}(Q_{T})
. Furthermore, even with
\nabla V\in L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}(Q_{T})
,
uniqueness is not clear if
q<m
or
p<2
. Therefore, those questions remain open.

2.3
An application

In this subsection, we provide an application to which our results can be properly referred.
We consider an repulsion type of Keller-Segel equations, which is
of the form

\rho_{t}-\Delta\rho^{m}=\nabla\cdot\left(\rho\nabla c\right),\qquad c_{t}-\Delta c=\rho,\quad\text{in }\ Q_{T}:={\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T],

(2.25)

where
d\geq 2
and
0<T<\infty
.
If (
2.25
) is considered in a bounded domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
no flux boundary condition is typically assgined, that is

\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial c}{\partial\nu}=0\qquad\mbox{ on }\,\,\partial\Omega.

(2.26)

One can compare to the porous medium type of classical Keller-Segel equations, which is a coupled system of the form

\rho_{t}-\Delta\rho^{m}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho\nabla c\right),\qquad c_{t}-\Delta c=\rho,\quad\text{in }\ Q_{T}.

(2.27)

Contrast to (
2.25
), it shows aggregation effect due to the opposite sign of reaction-diffusion term for the equation
\rho
, and it turns out that solutions globally exist if

m>\frac{2(d-1)}{d}
or blow-ups can occur in a finite time if
1<m\leq\frac{2(d-1)}{d}
(see e.g.
[
30
]
,
[
51
]
and reference therein). We remark that, in general, the linear case (
m=1
) with large data yields blow-up in a finite time except for one dimensional case (see e.g.
[
34
,
31
,
32
,
55
,
56
,
57
]
and reference therein).

In the case that
m=\frac{2(d-1)}{d}
and equation of
c
in (
2.27
) is of elliptic type, asymptotic behaviors of solutions were classified in
[
10
]
by using the gradient flow structure of the equation for each case of subcritical, critical or supercritical mass.
The free energy functional was analyzed in
[
13
]
to show that global minimizers are radial and compactly supported, and, in particular, the minimizer is unique in two dimensions and solutions of PME Keller-Segel equations asymptotically converges to the minimizer (see also the survey paper
[
14
]
).
Meanwhile, the existence of global
L^{q}
-weak solutions,
q>1
, was established in
[
9
]
, provided that
L^{p}
-norm,
p=\frac{d(2-m)}{2}
, of initial data is sufficiently small.

Since the equation (
1.1
) is quite general, our results do not seem to be directly applicable to those mentioned above for specific system (
2.27
).
Our analysis, however, provide a heuristic evidence why
p=\frac{d(2-m)}{2}
appears in
[
9
]
.
Suppose that a priori
L^{q}
-energy estimate is available. Then,
\rho\in L^{\frac{d(m+q-1)}{d-2},m+q-1}_{x,t}
, so is
\Delta c
due to
-\Delta c=\rho
. Applying the subscaling invariant condition for
\nabla V
(in this case
\nabla V=\Delta c
), we can conclude that
L^{q}
-weak solutions exist, provided that
q
satisfies

\frac{d+q_{m,d}}{m+q-1}\leq 2+q_{m,d}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad q\geq\frac{d(2-m)}{2}.

On the other hand, it was also shown in
[
9
]
that,
if no smallness restriction is assumed, blow-up in a finite time may occur. More precisely,
\lim_{t\to T^{\ast}}\left\|\nabla c(t)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}=\lim_{t\to T^{\ast}}\left\|\rho(t)\right\|_{L^{r}_{x}}=\infty
for any
r>p
, where
T^{\ast}
is a finite blow-up time. Our results can be also used to see that blow-up properties are relevant to each other. Indeed, suppose
\left\|\nabla c(t)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}
is finite until the blow-up time. Then, via the subscaling invariant condition, we can have global
L^{q}
-weak solution wtih
q
satisfying

q\leq\frac{2d(m-1)}{d-2}
.
If
m\geq\frac{2d}{d+2}
, then there, however, exists
q\in(\frac{d(2-m)}{2},\frac{2d(m-1)}{d-2}]
so that
L^{q}
-weak solution exists globally in time, which is contrary to the fact that
L^{r}
-norm of
\rho
for
r>p
becomes infinite at time
T^{\ast}
. Therefore,
\left\|\nabla c(t)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}
must be unbounded if
\lim_{t\to T^{\ast}}\|\rho(t)\|_{L^{r}_{x}}=\infty
for
r>p
.
Our results do not give any proof of the results in
[
9
]
, but it could be used to conduct heuristic observations.

Now we return to the system (
2.25
) of repulsive type.
Due to repusive effect of reaction diffusion term in (
2.25
), it is, however, unknown whether or not blow-up can occur in a finite time.
More precisely, the repulsive effect deduces via cancelation property the following estimate:

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\log\rho+\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left|\nabla c\right|^{2}\right)+\frac{4}{m^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right|^{2}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left|\Delta c\right|^{2}=0,

(2.28)

which is not available to the case of classical Keller-Segel equations (
2.27
).

It is not difficult to see that the a priori estimate (
2.28
) yields existence of entropy weak solutions. However, it is not simple to show whether or not such weak solutions are regular.
Similar phenomena can be found in the Nernst-Planck-Navier-Stokes system, which has a type of repulsive structure as in (
2.25
). We remark that
[
19
]
established global well-posedness of regular solutions in two
dimensions and
[
45
]
proved the existence of weak solutions for
two-ionic species by using free energy dissipation via gradient
flow structure in three dimensions.
In this directions, lots of improvements have recently been accomplished and we do not make a list of all relavent results here.

Our aim in this subsection is to improve the result of
[
28
]
with the aid of our main results.
As far as authors know, the best known results in regularity theory for (
2.25
)-(
2.26
) is that if
m>1+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{d^{2}}
,
d\geq 2
, then solutions become bounded globally in time (see
[
28
]
). We remark that note that
1+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{d^{2}}<\frac{2(d-1)}{d}
.
Our main objective is to show that solutions for (
2.25
) become globally bounded for the case
m>\max\{\frac{2d-3}{d},\,\frac{2d}{d+2}\}
.
We note, due to previous result in
[
28
]
, that it suffices to consider the case
\max\{\frac{2d-3}{d},\,\frac{2d}{d+2}\}<m\leq 1+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{d^{2}}
for
d>2
.
With the aid of our main results, we obtain the following.
The proof will be given in Section
7
.

Theorem 2.21

Let
m>\max\{\frac{2d-3}{d},\,\frac{2d}{d+2}\}
,
d>2
, and
p\in(1,\frac{md-d+2}{d-1}]
. Suppose that
\rho_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
c_{0}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Then, a pair of solution
(\rho,c)
for the equations (
2.25
) becomes bounded globally in time.

Remark 2.22

(i)

We remark that
m>\frac{2d-3}{d}
is equivalent to
\frac{md-d+2}{d-1}>1
. Moreover, we note that the condition
\max\{\frac{2d-3}{d},\,\frac{2d}{d+2}\}
can be rewritten as
\frac{2d}{d+2}\cdot\delta_{\{d\leq 6\}}+\frac{2d-3}{d}\cdot\delta_{\{d>6\}}
.

(ii)

If
\rho_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is assumed to be Hölder continuous, then so is
\rho
for
t\in(0,T]
. Since its proof is rather straightforward once solutions become bounded (see e.g.
[
17
]
), we omit the details. The uniqueness of solutions, however, remains open, since the results in Section
2.2
do not seem to be applicable to the system (
2.25
).

3
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce preliminaries that are used throughout the paper.

For a function
f:\Omega\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},d\geq 2
and constants
q_{1},q_{2}>1
, we define

\|f\|_{L^{q_{1},q_{2}}_{x,t}}:=\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left[\int_{\Omega}\left|f(x,t)\right|^{q_{1}}\,dx\right]^{q_{2}/q_{1}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{2}}}.

For simplicity, let
\|f\|_{L^{q}_{x,t}}=\|f\|_{L^{q,q}_{x,t}}
for some
q>1
. Also we denote for
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\Omega_{T}\subseteq Q_{T}
that

\begin{gathered}\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(\Omega)}:=\sup_{x,y\in\Omega,\,x\neq y}\frac{|f(x,t)-f(y,t)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}},\\
\|f\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(\Omega_{T})}:=\sup_{(x,t),(y,s)\in\Omega_{T},\,(x,t)\neq(y,s)}\frac{|f(x,t)-f(y,s)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}+|t-s|^{\alpha/2}}.\end{gathered}

3.1
Technical lemmas

We introduce well known Sobolev type inequalities. Throughout this subsection, let
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Lemma 3.1

[
29
, Theorem 7.10]

Let
\rho\in W_{o}^{1,p}(\Omega)
and assume
1\leq p<d
. There exists a constant
c=c(d,p)
such that

\|\rho\|_{L_{x}^{\frac{dp}{d-p}}(\Omega)}\leq c\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\Omega)}.

Lemma 3.2

[
26
, Proposition I.3.1]

Let
\rho\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{q}(\Omega))\cap L^{p}(0,T;W_{o}^{1,p}(\Omega))
for some
1\leq p<d
, and
0<q<\frac{dp}{d-p}
. Then there exists a constant
c=c(d,p,q)
such that

\iint_{\Omega_{T}}|\rho(x,t)|^{\frac{p(d+q)}{d}}\,dx\,dt\leq c\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{\Omega}|\rho(x,t)|^{q}\,dx\right)^{\frac{p}{d}}\iint_{\Omega_{T}}|\nabla\rho(x,t)|^{p}\,dx\,dt.

The following lemma is the Garilardo-Niremberg multiplicative embedding inequality.

Lemma 3.3

[
26
, Theorem I.2.1]

Let
\rho\in W_{o}^{1,p}(\Omega)
for
p\geq 1
. For every fixed number
s\geq 1
, there exists a constant
c=c(d,p,s)
such that

\|\rho\|_{L_{x}^{q}(\Omega)}\leq c\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\Omega)}^{\alpha}\|\rho\|_{L^{s}_{x}(\Omega)}^{1-\alpha},

where
\alpha\in[0,1]
,
p,q\geq 1
, are linked by

\alpha=\left(\frac{1}{s}-\frac{1}{q}\right)\left(\frac{1}{d}-\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{s}\right)^{-1}
,
and their admissible range is:

\begin{cases}s\leq q\leq\infty,\ 0\leq\alpha\leq\frac{p}{p+s(p-1)},&\text{ if }\ d=1,\\
s\leq q\leq\frac{dp}{d-p},\ 0\leq\alpha\leq 1,&\text{ if }\ 1\leq p<d,\ s\leq\frac{dp}{d-p},\\
\frac{dp}{d-p}\leq q\leq s,\ 0\leq\alpha\leq 1,&\text{ if }\ 1\leq p<d,\ s\geq\frac{dp}{d-p},\\
s\leq q<\infty,\ 0\leq\alpha<\frac{dp}{dp+s(p-d)},&\text{ if }\ p\geq d>1.\end{cases}

Now we derive the following lemma which is useful to obtain a priori estimates.

Lemma 3.4

Let
m>1
and
q\geq 1
. Suppose that

\rho\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{q}(\Omega))\quad\text{and}\quad\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)).

(3.1)

Then
\rho\in L^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{x,t}(\Omega\times[0,T])
such that

\begin{gathered}\frac{d}{r_{1}}+\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{r_{2}}=\frac{d}{q},\quad\text{for}\quad q_{m,d}:=\frac{d(m-1)}{q},\\
\text{for}\ \ \begin{cases}q\leq r_{1}\leq\frac{d(q+m-1)}{d-2},\quad q+m-1\leq r_{2}\leq\infty,&\text{ if }\ d>2,\\
q\leq r_{1}<\infty,\quad q+m-1<r_{2}\leq\infty,&\text{ if }\ d=2,\\
q\leq r_{1}\leq\infty,\quad q+m-1\leq r_{2}\leq\infty,&\text{ if }\ d=1.\end{cases}\end{gathered}

(3.2)

Moreover, there exists a constant
c=c(d)
such that, for
(d-2)_{+}=\max\{0,d-2\}
,

\|\rho\|_{L^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{x,t}}\leq c\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{q}(\cdot,t)\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{r_{1}}\left[1-\frac{(r_{1}-q)(d-2)_{+}}{d(m-1)+2q}\right]}\,\left\|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,t}}^{\frac{2}{r_{2}}}.

(3.3)

Proof.

First, let
d>2
. By applying Lemma
3.1
with (
3.1
), we let

q\leq r_{1}\leq\frac{d(q+m-1)}{d-2},\quad\frac{1}{r_{1}}=\frac{\theta}{q}+\frac{(1-\theta)(d-2)}{(q+m-1)d}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\theta=\frac{q}{r_{1}}\left(1-\frac{(r_{1}-q)(d-2)}{2q+d(m-1)}\right).

(3.4)

Then we carry the following computations

\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{r_{1}}_{x}}\leq\left\|\rho\right\|^{\theta}_{L^{q}_{x}}\left\|\rho\right\|^{1-\theta}_{L^{\frac{(q+m-1)d}{d-2}}_{x}}\leq\left\|\rho\right\|^{\theta}_{L^{q}_{x}}\left\|\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right\|^{\frac{2(1-\theta)}{q+m-1}}_{L^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}_{x}}\leq c\left\|\rho\right\|^{\theta}_{L^{q}_{x}}\left\|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right\|^{\frac{2(1-\theta)}{q+m-1}}_{L^{2}_{x}}.

(3.5)

By taking
L^{r_{2}}_{t}
, we obtain

\|\rho\|_{L^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{x,t}}\leq c\left\|\rho\right\|^{\theta}_{L^{q,\infty}_{x,t}}\left\|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right\|^{\frac{2}{r_{2}}}_{L^{2}_{x,t}},

provided

\frac{(1-\theta)r_{2}}{q+m-1}=1\quad\iff\quad\frac{d}{r_{1}}+\frac{2+q_{m,d}}{r_{2}}=\frac{d}{q}.

Now let
1\leq d\leq 2
, then Lemma
3.3
with
v=\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}
and
s=\frac{2q}{q+m-1}
gives that

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{\gamma\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\leq c\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{2}\,dx\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{q}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1-\alpha}{s}},

for
\alpha=\frac{\gamma-s}{\gamma}
and
\frac{2q}{q+m-1}\leq\gamma<\infty
. Then we set

r_{1}=q\theta+\gamma\frac{q+m-1}{2}(1-\theta),\quad\text{for}\quad\theta=1-\frac{2(r_{1}-q)}{\gamma(q+m-1)-q}\in[0,1].

Therefore, the combination of Hölder inequality for
\theta+(1-\theta)=1
yield

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{r_{1}}\,dx\leq c\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{q}\,dx\right)^{\theta+\frac{\theta\gamma(1-\alpha)}{s}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{2}\,dx\right)^{(1-\theta)\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}.

Then by taking power by
\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}
for
r_{2}>1
, we set
(1-\alpha)\frac{\alpha\gamma r_{2}}{2r_{1}}=1
which gives (
3.2
) and complete the proof.

Now we introduce the Aubin-Lions lemma.

Lemma 3.5

[
48
, Proposition III.1.3]
,
[
50
]

Let
X_{0}
,
X
and
X_{1}
be Banach spaces with
X_{0}\subset X\subset X_{1}
. Suppose that
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are reflexive, and
X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
is compact, and
X\hookrightarrow X_{1}
is continuous. For
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
, let us define

W=\left\{u\in L^{p}\left(0,T;X_{0}\right),\ u_{t}\in L^{q}\left(0,T;X_{1}\right)\right\}
.
If
p<\infty
, then the inclusion
W\hookrightarrow L^{p}(0,T;X)
is compact. If
p=\infty
and
q>1
, then the embedding of
W
into
{\mathcal{C}}(0,T;X)
is compact.

3.2
Wasserstein space

In this subsection, we introduce the Wasserstein space and its properties. For more detail, we refer
[
1
,
54
]
.

Definition 3.6

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Suppose there is a measurable map
\mathcal{T}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
then
\mathcal{T}
induces a probability measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

which is defined as

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\,d\nu(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(\mathcal{T}(x))\,d\mu(x),\qquad\forall~\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We denote
\nu:=\mathcal{T}_{\#}\mu
and say that
\nu
is the push-forward of
\mu
by
\mathcal{T}
.

Let us denote by
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the set of all Borel
probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a finite
p
-th moment. For
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we consider

W_{p}(\mu,\nu):=\left(\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{p}\,d\gamma(x,y)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

(3.6)

where
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
denotes the set of all Borel probability
measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
which has
\mu
and

\nu
as marginals;

\gamma(A\times\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mu(A)\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A)=\nu(A)

for every Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Equation (
3.6
) defines a distance on

\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is called the
Wasserstein distance
.
Equipped with the Wasserstein distance,
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is called the
Wasserstein space
.
It is known that the infimum in the right hand side of Equation
(
3.6
) always achieved.

We will denote by
\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)
the set of all
\gamma
which minimize the expression.
If
\mu
is
absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure then there exists a map
\mathcal{T}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that

\gamma:=(Id\times\mathcal{T})_{\#}\mu
is the unique element of
\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)
, that is,
\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)=\{(Id\times\mathcal{T})_{\#}\mu\}
.

We say that a sequence
\{\mu_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is narrowly convergent to
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

n\rightarrow\infty
if

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,d\mu_{n}(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,d\mu(x)

(3.7)

for every function
\varphi\in{\mathcal{C}}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, the space of continuous and bounded real functions defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Then we recall

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}W_{p}(\mu_{n},\mu)=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\begin{cases}\mu_{n}~\mbox{narrowly converges to }\mu~\mbox{ in }\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,d\mu_{n}(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,d\mu(x).\end{cases}

Following
[
2
,
1
]
, we give a notion of a differential and a definition of convex functions on
\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

Definition 3.7

Let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mapsto(-\infty,\infty]
.
We say that
\phi
is geodesically convex in
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if for every
couple
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists an
optimal plan
\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
such that

\phi(\mu_{t}^{1\rightarrow 2})\leq(1-t)\phi(\mu_{1})+t\phi(\mu_{2}),\qquad\forall~t\in[0,1],

where
\mu_{t}^{1\rightarrow 2}
is a constant speed geodesic
between
\mu^{1}
and
\mu^{2}
defined as

\mu_{t}^{1\rightarrow 2}:=((1-t)\pi^{1}+t\pi^{2})_{\#}\gamma.

Here,
\pi^{1}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\pi^{2}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
are the first and second projections of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
onto
\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by

\pi^{1}(x,y)=x,\quad\pi^{2}(x,y)=y,\qquad\forall~x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Definition 3.8

Let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\rightarrow(-\infty,\infty]
be a proper function on
\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, i.e. the effective domain of
\phi
defined by

D(\phi):=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}):\phi(\mu)<\infty\}
is not empty. We say that

\xi\in L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(\mu)
belongs to the subdifferential
\partial_{-}\phi(\mu)
if

\phi(\nu)\geq\phi(\mu)+\sup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle\xi(x),y-x\rangle d\gamma(x,y)+o(W_{p}(\mu,\nu)),

as
\nu\rightarrow\mu
in
\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

Lemma 3.9

[
2
, Proposition 4.2]

Let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\rightarrow(-\infty,\infty]
be lower semicontinuous and geodesically convex and let
\xi\in\partial_{-}\phi(\mu)
. Then
we have

\phi(\nu)\geq\phi(\mu)+\sup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle\xi(x),y-x\rangle d\gamma(x,y),\qquad\forall~\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

Now, we introduce the notion of absolutely continuous curve and its relation with the continuity equation.

Definition 3.10

Let
\sigma:[0,T]\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a curve.
We say that
\sigma
is absolutely continuous and denote it by
\sigma\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, if there exists
l\in L^{1}([0,T])
such that

W_{p}(\sigma(s),\sigma(t))\leq\int_{s}^{t}l(r)dr,\qquad\forall~0\leq s\leq t\leq T.

(3.8)

If
\sigma\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, then the limit

|\sigma^{\prime}|(t):=\lim_{s\rightarrow t}\frac{W_{p}(\sigma(s),\sigma(t))}{|s-t|},

exists for
L^{1}
-a.e
t\in[0,T]
. Moreover, the function
|\sigma^{\prime}|
belongs to
L^{1}(0,T)
and satisfies

|\sigma^{\prime}|(t)\leq l(t)\qquad\mbox{for}~L^{1}\mbox{-a.e.}~t\in[0,T],

for any
l
satisfying (
3.8
).
We call
|\sigma^{\prime}|
by the metric derivative of
\sigma
.

Lemma 3.11

[
1
, Theorem 8.3.1]

If
\sigma\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
then there exists a Borel vector field
w:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T)\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that

w(t)\in L^{p}(\sigma(t)),\quad\|w(t)\|_{L^{p}(\sigma(t))}\leq|\sigma^{\prime}|(t),\quad\mbox{ for}~~L^{1}\text{-a.e}~~t\in[0,T],

and the continuity equation

\partial_{t}\sigma+\nabla\cdot(w\,\sigma)=0,

holds in the sense of distribution.
Conversely, if a narrowly continuous curve
\sigma:[0,T]\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies the continuity equation
for some Borel vector field
w
with
\|w(t)\|_{L^{p}(\sigma(t))}\in L^{1}(0,T)
, then
\sigma:[0,T]\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is absolutely continuous and

|\sigma^{\prime}|(t)\leq\|w(t)\|_{L^{p}(\sigma(t))}
for
L^{1}
-a.e
t\in[0,T]
.

Remark 3.12

We call
\|w(t)\|_{L^{p}(\sigma(t))}
an auxiliary speed of curve
\sigma(t)
in Wasserstein space
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We use ‘speed’ instead of ‘auxiliary speed’ unless any confusion is to be expected.

Lemma 3.13

[
1
, Proposition 3.3.1]

Let
K\subset\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be a sequentially compact set w.r.t the narrow topology.
Let
\sigma_{n}:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be curves such that

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}(t)\in K,\quad\forall~n

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{N},~t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle W_{p}(\sigma_{n}(s),\sigma_{n}(t))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(s,t),\qquad\forall~0\leq s\leq t\leq T,~n\in\mathbb{N},

for a continuous function
\omega:[0,T]\times[0,T]\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that

\omega(t,t)=0,~~\forall~t\in[0,T].

Then there exists a subsequnece
\sigma_{n_{k}}
and a limit curve
\sigma:[0,T]\rightarrow{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\sigma_{n_{k}}(t)\mbox{ narrowly converges to}~~\sigma(t),\qquad\text{for all}~~t\in[0,T].

3.3
Flows on
\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
generated by vector fields

For a given
T>0
, let
V\in L^{1}(0,T;W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d};{\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
. For any
s,~t\in[0,T]
, let
\psi:[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the flow map of the vector field
V
.
More precisely,
\psi
solves the following ODE

\begin{cases}\frac{d}{dt}\psi(t;s,x)=V(\psi(t;s,x),t),&\text{for }s,t\in[0,T]\\
\psi(s;s,x)=x,&\text{for }x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.\end{cases}

(3.9)

Using the flow map
\psi
, we define a flow
\Psi:[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
through the push forward operation as follows

\Psi(t;s,\mu):={\psi}(t;s,\cdot)_{\#}\mu,\qquad\forall~\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

(3.10)

In this subsection, we remind two basic results on the flow map
\psi
.

Lemma 3.14

[
35
, Lemma 3.1]

Let
s\in[0,T]
and
\psi
be defined as in (
3.9
). Then, for any
t\in[s,T]
, the map
\psi(t;s,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies

e^{-\int_{s}^{t}\mbox{Lip}(V(\tau))\,d\tau}|x-y|\leq|\psi(t;s,x)-\psi(t;s,y)|\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}\mbox{Lip}(V(\tau))\,d\tau}|x-y|,\qquad\forall~x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where

\mbox{Lip}(V(\tau)):=\sup_{x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\,x\neq y}\frac{|V(x,\tau)-V(y,\tau)|}{|x-y|}.

Lemma 3.15

Let
s\in[0,T]
and
\psi
be defined as in (
3.9
). For any
t\in[s,T]
, let
J_{s,t}
be the Jacobian corresponding to the map

\psi(t;s,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
. That is,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta(y)dy:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta(\psi(t;s,x))J_{s,t}(x)\,dx,\qquad\forall~\zeta\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(3.11)

Then, the Jacobian
J_{s,t}
is given as

J_{s,t}(x)=e^{\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;s,x),\tau)\,d\tau},\qquad\forall~x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

(3.12)

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be given. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}J_{s,t}(x)\big|_{t=\tau}

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;s,x),\tau)J_{s,\tau}(x).

Since
J_{s,s}\equiv 1
, we have

\log J_{s,t}(x)=\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;s,x),\tau)\,d\tau.

(3.13)

This completes the proof.

Remark 3.16

We note that
\psi(t;s,\cdot)\circ\psi(s;t,\cdot)=Id
, that is,

\psi(t;s,\psi(s;t,x))=x,\quad\forall~x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

Exploiting this, we have

J_{s,t}(\psi(s;t,x))=\frac{1}{J_{t,s}(x)}.

Lemma 3.17

[
35
, Lemma 3.3]

Let
s\in[0,T]
and
\Psi
be in (
3.10
). For any
t\in[s,T]
and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
we have

e^{-\int_{s}^{t}\mbox{Lip}(V(\tau))\,d\tau}W_{p}(\mu,\nu)\leq W_{p}(\Psi(t;s,\mu),\Psi(t;s,\nu))\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}\mbox{Lip}(V(\tau))\,d\tau}W_{p}(\mu,\nu).

(3.14)

Lemma 3.18

[
35
, Lemma 3.4]

Let
\psi
and
\Psi
be defined as in (
3.9
) and (
3.10
), respectively. If
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
then

\Psi(t;s,\mu)\in\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Furthermore, suppose
\mu=\varrho\,dx
and
\Psi(t;s,\mu)=\rho\,dx
then

\rho(\psi(t;s,x))J_{s,t}(x)=\varrho(x),\qquad a.e\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

(3.15)

where
J_{s,t}
is the Jacobian of the map
\psi(t;s,\cdot)
as in (
3.11
).
We also have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\log\rho\,dx=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\varrho\log\varrho\,dx-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\varrho\log J_{s,t}\,dx.

(3.16)

Moreover, if
\varrho\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
q\in[1,\infty]
, then

{\rho}\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and we have

\|{\rho}\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\|\varrho\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}e^{\frac{q-1}{q}\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau},

(3.17)

where
\frac{q-1}{q}=1
if
q=\infty
.

Lemma 3.19

Let

\psi
and
\Psi
be defined as in (
3.9
) and (
3.10
), respectively. Suppose that
V\in L^{1}(0,T;W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
.

(i)

If
V\in L^{1}(0,T;{\mathcal{C}}^{1,\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
and
\varrho\in\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
then
\rho:=\Psi(t;s,\varrho)
is also Hölder continuous. More
precisely, we have
\|\rho\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}<C
where
C=C(\|\varrho\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})},\,\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla V\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}d\tau)
.

(ii)

If
V\in L^{1}(0,T;W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
, then, for any
a>0,~q\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla\rho^{a}\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq e^{(a+2)\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}\left\{\|\nabla\varrho^{a}\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\|\varrho^{a}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\times\left(a\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla^{2}V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau\right)\right\}.

(3.18)

Proof.

(i)
From (
3.15
), we have

\rho(x)=\frac{{\varrho}(\psi(s;t,x))}{J_{s,t}(\psi(s;t,x))}.

(3.19)

Let
f:=\varrho(\psi(s;t,x))
and
g:=\frac{1}{J_{s,t}(\psi(s;t,x))}
, then

\|\rho\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\|f\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

We note that

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}=\|\varrho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

and from (
3.12
)

\|g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}.

Next, we estimate
\|f\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}
that

|\varrho(\psi(s;t,x))-\varrho(\psi(s;t,y))|\leq\|\varrho\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}}|\psi(s;t,x)-\psi(s;t,y)|^{\alpha}\leq\|\varrho\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}}e^{\alpha\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}|x-y|^{\alpha}.

Hence, it gives

\|f\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}}\leq\|\varrho\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}}e^{\alpha\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}.

Next, we note that

\displaystyle g(x)=e^{-\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;s,\psi(s;t,x)),\tau)\,d\tau}=e^{-\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,x),\tau)\,d\tau},

which gives us

\displaystyle|g(x)-g(y)|

\displaystyle=\left|e^{-\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,x),\tau)\,d\tau}-e^{-\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,y),\tau)\,d\tau}\right|

(3.20)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}\int_{s}^{t}|\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,x),\tau)-\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,y),\tau)|\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V(\tau)\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}}|\psi(\tau;t,x)-\psi(\tau;t,y)|^{\alpha}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V(\tau)\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}}\,d\tau~e^{\alpha\int_{s}^{t}Lip(V(\theta))d\theta}|x-y|^{\alpha}.

We combine (
3.19
)-(
3.20
) to get, for
any
x,~y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\displaystyle|\rho(x)-\rho(y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{(\alpha+1)\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}\left(\|\varrho\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla\cdot V(\tau)\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}}\,d\tau+\|\varrho\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}}\right)|x-y|^{\alpha}.

(ii)
First of all, we exploit

\psi(\tau;s,\psi(s;t,x))=\psi(\tau;t,x)
in (
3.19
)
and get

\displaystyle\rho(x)=\varrho(\psi(s;t,x))~e^{-\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,x),\tau)\,d\tau}.

Hence, for
a>1
, we have

\displaystyle\rho^{a}(x)=\varrho^{a}(\psi(s;t,x))~e^{-a\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,x),\tau)\,d\tau},

and

\displaystyle\nabla\rho^{a}(x)

\displaystyle=\nabla\varrho^{a}(\psi(s;t,x))~\nabla_{x}\psi(s;t,x)~e^{-a\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,x),\tau)\,d\tau}

(3.21)

\displaystyle+\varrho^{a}(\psi(s;t,x))~e^{-a\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\cdot V(\psi(\tau;t,x)\,d\tau)}\left(-a\int_{s}^{t}\nabla^{2}V(\psi(\tau;t,x))\nabla_{x}\psi(\tau;t,x)\,d\tau\right).

From Lemma
3.14
, we note

\left|\nabla_{x}\psi(s;t,x)\right|\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}.

(3.22)

We combine (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) to get

\displaystyle\left|\nabla\rho^{a}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\left|\nabla\varrho^{a}(\psi(s;t,x))\right|+\varrho^{a}(\psi(s;t,x))\times\left(a\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla^{2}V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau\right)\right\}e^{(a+1)\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}.

(3.23)

Next, we note that for any nonnegative function
f

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(\psi(s;t,x))dx=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(y)J_{t,s}(y)\,dy\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla V\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\,d\tau}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(y)\,dy.

(3.24)

Combining (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), we have (
3.18
).

4
A priori estimates

Here we provide a priori estimates of a regular solution of (
1.1
) given as following definition.

Definition 4.1

Let
q\in[1,\infty)
and
V
be a measurable vector field.
We say that a nonnegative measurable function
\rho
is
a
regular solution
of (
1.1
) with
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
if the followings are satisfied:

(i)

It holds that

\rho\in L^{1}(Q_{T})\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\quad\nabla\rho^{\frac{m+q-1}{2}}\in L^{2}(Q_{T}),\quad\text{and}\quad V\in L^{1}_{\loc}(Q_{T}).

(ii)

For any
\varphi\in{\mathcal{C}}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[0,T))
, it holds that

\iint_{Q_{T}}\left\{\rho\varphi_{t}-\nabla\rho^{m}\cdot\nabla\varphi+\rho V\cdot\nabla\varphi\right\}\,dx\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{0}\varphi(\cdot,0)\,dx.

By following similar arguments as in
[
53
, Proposition 9.15]
, for
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have that a regular solution
\rho
of (
1.1
) carries the mass conservation property in time; that is,
\|\rho(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1
a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.
Throughout this section, positive constants
m
,
d
,
p
,
q
, and
T
are given, hence we omit the dependence of
c
and
C
, unless we need to specify the dependencies clearly.

4.1
Supplementary estimates of speed and
p
-th moment

Note that (
1.1
) can be written as

\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla\cdot\left(w\rho\right)=0,\quad\text{where}\quad w:=-\frac{\nabla\rho^{m}}{\rho}+V.

(4.1)

Due to Lemma
3.11
, (
4.1
) can be seen as a curve in
{\mathcal{P}}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
whose speed at time
t
is limited by
\|w(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\rho(t))}
. In the following lemma, we deliver the estimate of
\|w\|_{L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}(\rho(t)))}
.

Lemma 4.2

Let
m>1
,
1\leq q\leq m
, and
1<p\leq{\lambda_{q}}:=1+\frac{d(q-1)+q}{d(m-1)+q}
. Suppose that
\rho:Q_{T}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
is a nonnegative measurable function satisfying

\rho\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\quad\text{and}\quad\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

Furthermore, assume that

V\in\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\ \text{ for }\ \begin{cases}{\lambda_{q}}\leq q_{2}\leq\frac{{\lambda_{q}}(q+m-1)}{q+m-2},&\text{ if }\ d>2,\\
{\lambda_{q}}\leq q_{2}<\frac{{\lambda_{q}}(q+m-1)}{q+m-2},&\text{ if }\ d=2.\end{cases}

(4.2)

Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists constant
c=c(\varepsilon)
such that

\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\frac{\nabla\rho^{m}}{\rho}\right|^{p}\rho\,dx\,dt\leq\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{2}\,dx\,dt+cT\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{q}\,dx\right)^{\frac{2\theta}{d(1-\theta)}},

(4.3)

\iint_{Q_{T}}|V|^{p}\rho\,dx\,dt\leq\varepsilon\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{2}\,dx\,dt+cT^{\beta_{1}\frac{{\lambda_{q}}-p}{{\lambda_{q}}}}\|\rho\|_{L^{q,\infty}_{x,t}}^{\beta\beta_{1}\frac{qp(q_{1}-{\lambda_{q}})}{q_{1}{\lambda_{q}}}}\|V\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}}^{p\beta_{1}},

(4.4)

where
\theta=\frac{dp(m-q)}{(2-p)[(d+2)q+(m-2)d]}
,
\beta=1-\frac{(d-2)(q_{1}(1-q)+q{\lambda_{q}})}{(q_{1}-{\lambda_{q}})(d(m-1)+2q)}
, and
\beta_{1}=\frac{q_{2}{\lambda_{q}}}{q_{2}({\lambda_{q}}-p)+p{\lambda_{q}}}
.

Proof.

For simplicity, let
\lambda:=\lambda_{q}
.
First we prove (
4.3
). If
1\leq q<m
, then
1<p\leq{\lambda}<2
and we observe that

\left|\frac{\nabla\rho^{m}}{\rho}\right|^{p}\rho=\left(\frac{2m}{q+m-1}\right)^{p}\rho^{\frac{2-p+p(m-q)}{2}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{p}.

By applying Young’s inequality with
\frac{p}{2}+\frac{2-p}{2}=1
, we have the following, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\frac{\nabla\rho^{m}}{\rho}\right|^{p}\rho\,dx\,dt\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right|^{2}\,dx\,dt+c(\varepsilon)\iint_{Q_{T}}\rho^{\frac{2-p+p(m-q)}{2-p}}\,dx\,dt.

(4.5)

By Lemma
3.2
for
d>2
(or Lemma
3.3
, for
d=2
), it holds that
\rho\in L_{x,t}^{(q+m-1)+\frac{2q}{d}}
.
Therefore, we set

1\leq\frac{2-p+p(m-q)}{2-p}\leq q+m-1+\frac{2q}{d}\ \iff\ 1<p\leq 1+\frac{d(q-1)+q}{d(m-1)+q}.

To handle the second term on the RHS of (
4.5
), let us set

\frac{2-p+p(m-q)}{2-p}=(1-\theta)+\left(q+m-1+\frac{2q}{d}\right)\theta,\quad\text{for}\quad\theta\in(0,1).

Then the Hölder inequality and Lemma
3.2
yield

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{T}}\rho^{\frac{2-p+p(m-q)}{2-p}}\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho\|_{L_{x,t}^{1}}^{1-\theta}\|\rho\|_{L^{q,\infty}_{x,t}}^{\frac{2\theta q}{d}}\left\|\nabla\rho^{\frac{q+m-1}{2}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,t}}^{2\theta}

(4.6)
