(i)
.

7
Proofs of uniqueness and an application

In this section, we present proofs of Theorem
2.18
and Theorem
2.21
.
First, we recall a technical lemma, which is useful for the uniqueness result.

Lemma 7.1

[
37
, Lemma 2.4.1]

For
i=1,2
, let
\rho_{i}\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
and
w_{i}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\rho_{i}))
be the velocity for
\rho_{i}
, i.e

\partial_{t}\rho_{i}+\nabla\cdot(w_{i}\rho_{i})=0.

We set
f(t):=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{1}(t),\rho_{2}(t))
. Then,
f\in W^{1,2}(0,T)
and

f^{\prime}(t)\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle w_{1}(x,t)-w_{2}(y,t),x-y\rangle\,d\gamma_{t}(x,y),\quad\gamma_{t}\in\Gamma_{0}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),

for a.e
t\in(0,T)
.

By exploiting above lemma, the next proposition provides a uniqueness result in the class of absolutely continuous curves in
\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

Proposition 7.2

Let
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
q\geq 1
. Suppose
that
\rho_{i}\in AC(0,T:\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
for
i=1,2
are
L^{q}
-weak solutions of (
1.1
)
satisfying

\iint_{Q_{T}}\left(\left|\frac{\nabla\rho_{i}^{m}}{\rho_{i}}\right|^{2}+|V|^{2}\right)\rho_{i}\,dx\,dt<\infty,\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2.

(7.1)

Assume further that
\nabla V\in L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}(Q_{T})
. Then they are identically the same, i.e.
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
.

Proof.

For
i=1,2
, let
\rho_{i}\in AC(0,T:\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
be a
L^{q}
-weak solution of

\partial_{t}\rho_{i}+\nabla\cdot(-\nabla\rho_{i}^{m}+V\rho_{i})=0,\quad\rho_{i}(\cdot,0)=\rho_{0}

(7.2)

satisfying (
7.1
).
Let
w_{i}:=-\frac{\nabla\rho_{i}^{m}}{\rho_{i}}+V
.
From (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), we note that
w_{i}
is the velocity for
\rho_{i}
and satisfies
w_{i}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\rho_{i}))
.
We define
f:[0,T]\rightarrow(-\infty,\infty)
by
f(t)=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{1}(t),\rho_{2}(t))
. Then, from Lemma
7.1
, we have
f\in W^{1,2}(0,T)
and

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle w_{1}(x,t)-w_{2}(y,t),x-y\rangle\,d\gamma_{t}(x,y)

(7.3)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle-\frac{\nabla\rho_{1}^{m}}{\rho_{1}}(x,t)+\frac{\nabla\rho_{2}^{m}}{\rho_{2}}(y,t),x-y\rangle\,d\gamma_{t}(x,y)+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle V(x,t)-V(y,t),x-y\rangle\,d\gamma_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=I+II,\qquad\text{ for a.e. }t\in(0,T).

To estimate
I
, we first note
\rho_{i}^{m}\in L^{1}(0,T;W^{1,1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
for
i=1,2
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\nabla\rho_{i}^{m}(x,t)\right|\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle=\iint_{Q_{T}}\frac{|\nabla\rho_{i}^{m}|}{\rho_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\rho_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,dx\,dt\leq\left(\iint_{Q_{T}}\frac{|\nabla\rho_{i}^{m}|^{2}}{\rho_{i}}\,dx\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\iint_{Q_{T}}\rho_{i}\,dx\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=T^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\iint_{Q_{T}}\left|\frac{\nabla\rho_{i}^{m}}{\rho_{i}}\right|^{2}\rho_{i}\,dx\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty.

Especially, this means
\rho_{i}^{m}(\cdot,t)\in W^{1,1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for a.e
t\in[0,T]
and
i=1,2
.

Next, we define
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\rightarrow(-\infty,\infty]
by

\mathcal{F}(\mu)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{m}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho^{m}\,dx,&\text{ if }\mu=\rho\mathcal{L}^{d},\\
+\infty,&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{L}^{d}
is the Lebesgue measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Then,
\mathcal{F}
is geodesically convex in
\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
(refer Chapter 9 in
[
1
]
). Furthermore, if
\rho^{m}\in W^{1,1}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
then

\frac{\nabla\rho^{m}}{\rho}\in\partial_{-}\mathcal{F}(\rho)
(refer Section 10.4 in
[
1
]
). Hence, for a.e
t\in[0,T]
and
i=1,2
,
we have
\frac{\nabla(\rho_{i}(t))^{m}}{\rho_{i}(t)}\in\partial_{-}\mathcal{F}(\rho_{i}(t))
which means

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\rho_{2}(t))\geq\mathcal{F}(\rho_{1}(t))+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle\frac{\nabla\rho_{1}^{m}}{\rho_{1}}(x,t),y-x\rangle\,d\gamma_{t}(x,y),\quad~\gamma_{t}\in\Gamma_{0}(\rho_{1}(t),\rho_{2}(t))

(7.4)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\rho_{1}(t))\geq\mathcal{F}(\rho_{2}(t))+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle\frac{\nabla\rho_{2}^{m}}{\rho_{2}}(x,t),y-x\rangle\,d\tilde{\gamma}_{t}(x,y),\quad~\tilde{\gamma}_{t}\in\Gamma_{0}(\rho_{2}(t),\rho_{1}(t)),

(7.5)

where we note that
\Gamma_{0}(\rho_{1}(t),\rho_{2}(t))
and
\Gamma_{0}(\rho_{2}(t),\rho_{1}(t))
consist of single element due to the fact that

\rho_{1}(t)
and
\rho_{2}(t)
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
By adding (
7.4
) and (
7.5
), we have

\displaystyle 0\geq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle\frac{\nabla\rho_{1}^{m}}{\rho_{1}}(x,t)-\frac{\nabla\rho_{2}^{m}}{\rho_{2}}(y,t),y-x\rangle\,d\gamma_{t}(x,y)=I.

(7.6)

For the estimation of
II
, we exploit
\nabla V\in L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}(\bar{Q}_{T})
to have

II\leq\|\nabla V(\cdot,t)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|x-y|^{2}\,d\gamma_{t}(x,y)=\|\nabla V(\cdot,t)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{1}(t),\rho_{2}(t)).

(7.7)

We plug (
7.6
) and (
7.7
) into (
7.3
) to have

f^{\prime}(t)\leq\|\nabla V(\cdot,t)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}f(t)\quad for\quad a.e~~t\in[0,T],

which gives us

W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{1}(t),\rho_{2}(t))\leq\|\nabla V\|_{L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}}W_{2}^{2}(\rho_{1}(0),\rho_{2}(0))\quad\forall~t\in[0,T].

Since
\rho_{1}(0)=\rho_{2}(0)=\rho_{0}
, we have
\rho_{1}(t)=\rho_{2}(t)
for all
t\in[0,T]
. This completes the proof.

With the aid of Proposition
7.2
, we are ready to prove Theorem
2.18
.

Proof of Theorem
2.18

:
For
i=1,~2
, let
\rho_{i}
be a
L^{m}
-weak solution of (
7.2
) such that
\rho_{i}:[0,T]\mapsto\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is narrowly continuous.
Hence, we have

\|\rho_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{m}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))}+\|\nabla\rho_{i}^{\frac{2m-1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})}<\infty,\quad\mbox{for}~~i=1,~2.

(7.8)

We recall that
V
satisfies (
2.24
) which is (
4.2
) for
q=m
and
{\lambda_{q}}=2
. Once we have (
7.8
) and (
4.2
), we exploit Lemma
4.2
and Remark
4.4
to have
the speed estimate and the moment estimate. More precisely, we have (
4.3
), (
4.4
) and (
4.22
) for
p=2
.
Hence, exploiting Grönwall’s inequality as in the proof of Proposition
4.10
, we get

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{t\}}\rho_{i}\langle x\rangle^{2}\,dx+\iint_{Q_{T}}\left(\left|\frac{\nabla\rho_{i}^{m}}{\rho_{i}}\right|^{2}+|V|^{2}\right)\rho_{i}\,dx\,dt<\infty,

which implies
\rho_{i}\in AC(0,T;\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
for

i=1,2
.
Due to the assumption
\nabla V\in L_{x,t}^{\infty,1}(Q_{T})
, we exploit Proposition
7.2
to conclude the uniqueness which completes the proof.

Proof of Corollary
2.19
:
Since
\rho_{0}\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
p\geq 2
and
q\geq m
, we have
\rho_{0}\in L^{m}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
Due to
q\geq m
, we first note
{\lambda_{q}}=2
. We also note the following relations in sub-scaling classes
\mathfrak{S}_{m,q}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{m,m}^{(q_{1},q_{2})}
and

\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,q}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{m,m}^{(\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2})}
.
Exploiting these inclusions, as in the proof of Proposition
4.10
, we have that if
V
satisfy the hypothesis
in one of Theorem
2.9
,
2.13
and
2.16
then
V
satisfies (
2.24
) which is (
4.2
) for
q=m
.
Hence, we exploit Theorem
2.18
and complete the proof.

Next, we provide the proof of Theorem
2.21
.

Proof of Theorem
2.21

: As mentioned before, we need to prove only the case
\max\{\frac{2d-3}{d},\,\frac{2d}{d+2}\}<m\leq 1+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{d^{2}}
for
d>2
due to previous result in
[
28
]
.
We first compute the
p
-th moment estimate, where
p>1
is determined later. Direct computations using Hölder inequality show that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho^{m}\Delta\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\nabla c\nabla\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx

(7.9)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho^{m}\Delta\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx+\epsilon\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\left|\nabla c\right|^{p}\,dx+C_{\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx.

For the first term in (
7.9
), we have (see also (
4.17
))

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho^{m}\Delta\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx\leq C\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{m}_{x}}^{m}\leq C\left\|\rho\right\|^{m\mu}_{L^{1}_{x}}\left\|\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right\|^{2(1-\mu)}_{L^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}_{x}}\leq C\left\|\nabla\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2(1-\mu)},

where
\mu=\frac{2}{d(m-1)+2}
.
On the other hand, the second term,
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\left|\nabla c\right|^{p}\,dx
, can be controlled as follows:

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\left|\nabla c\right|^{p}\,dx\leq\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{a}_{x}}\left\|\left|\nabla c\right|^{p}\right\|_{L^{b}_{x}}=\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{a}_{x}}\left\|\nabla c\right\|^{p}_{L^{bp}_{x}}\leq C\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{a}_{x}}\left\|\nabla^{2}c\right\|^{p}_{L^{a}_{x}},

where
a=\frac{(p+1)d}{p+d}
and
b=\frac{(p+1)d}{p(d-1)}
.
Therefore, we have for
\alpha=p+1
and
\beta=\frac{p+1}{p}

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\left|\nabla c\right|^{p}\,dxdt\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{a}_{x}}\left\|\nabla^{2}c\right\|^{p}_{L^{a}_{x}}\,dt\leq C\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{a,\alpha}_{x,t}}\left\|\nabla^{2}c\right\|^{p}_{L^{a,p\beta}_{x,t}}\leq C\left\|\rho\right\|^{p+1}_{L^{a,p+1}_{x,t}},

where we used the maximal regularity of
c
, i.e.
\left\|\nabla^{2}c\right\|_{L^{a,p+1}_{x,t}}\lesssim\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{a,p+1}_{x,t}}
(here we assume that the initial data
c_{0}(x)=0
for simplicity, since it is enough to add
\left\|c_{0}\right\|_{W^{2,a}_{x}}
in the righthand side, otherwise).
Noting that

\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{a}_{x}}\leq\left\|\rho\right\|^{\theta}_{L^{1}_{x}}\left\|\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right\|_{L^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}_{x}}^{\frac{2(1-\theta)}{m}},\quad\text{for }\ 1-\theta=\frac{pm(d-1)}{(p+1)(md-d+2)},

it follows that

\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\rho(t)\right\|^{p+1}_{L^{a}_{x}}\,dt\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{2(p+1)(1-\theta)}{m}}\,dt.

To set
\frac{(p+1)(1-\theta)}{m}\leq 1
, here we choose
p\in\left(1,\frac{md-d+2}{d-1}\right]
since
m>\frac{2d-3}{d}
. We can see that
any
p\in\left(1,\frac{md-d+2}{d-1}\right]
is less than
{\lambda_{1}}=1+\frac{1}{md-d+1}
, since
m<1+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{d^{2}}
(in fact, it is valid as long as
m<\frac{2(d-1)}{d}
).
Now integrating (
7.9
) in time and combining estimates above, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho(\cdot,t)\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho_{0}\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dx+C\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\nabla\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2(1-\mu)}\,d\tau

(7.10)

\displaystyle+C\epsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\nabla\rho^{\frac{m}{2}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{2(p+1)(1-\theta)}{m}}\,d\tau+C_{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho\langle x\rangle^{p}\,dxd\tau,

for any
t\in(0,T]
.
Combining estimates (
2.28
) and (
7.10
), and using Grönwall’s inequality, we can control
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho|\log\rho|\,dx
via (
4.28
), which
implies immediately that

\rho\in L^{1,\infty}_{x,t}\cap L^{\frac{md}{d-2},m}_{x,t}
,
and therefore

\rho\in L^{a,b}_{x,t},\quad\text{where}\quad\frac{d}{a}+\frac{2+d(m-1)}{b}=d,\quad 1\leq a\leq\frac{md}{d-2}.

In particular, in case
b=m
, it follows that

\rho\in L^{a,m}_{x,t}
with
a=\frac{md}{d-2}
.
Due to maximal regularity, one can see that

c_{t},\Delta c\in L^{a,m}_{x,t}
.
We claim that

\nabla c\in L^{q^{(1)}_{1}}_{x}L^{\frac{m}{m-1}}_{t}\quad\text{for}\quad q^{(1)}_{1}=\frac{md}{d+2-3m}.

Indeed,
recalling that
\rho\in L_{x}^{\frac{md}{d-2}}L^{m}_{t}
, it follows that for
q^{(1)}_{1}\geq\frac{md}{d-2}

\left\|\nabla c\right\|_{L^{q^{(1)}_{1}}_{x}}\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{t}(\nabla\Gamma*\rho)(\tau)d\tau\right\|_{L^{q^{(1)}_{1}}_{x}}\leq C\int_{0}^{t}(t-\tau)^{-\frac{d}{2}(\frac{d-2}{md}-\frac{1}{q^{(1)}_{1}})-\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\rho(\tau)\right\|_{L_{x}^{\frac{md}{d-2}}}\,d\tau.

Again using the Young’s inequality, we have

\left\|\nabla c\right\|_{L^{q^{(1)}_{1}}_{x}L^{\frac{m}{m-1}}_{t}}\leq C\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{\frac{md}{d-2},m}_{x,t}},\quad\text{for}\quad\frac{md}{d-2}\leq q^{(1)}_{1}\leq\frac{md}{d+2-3m}.

We note that
\nabla c\in S^{(q^{(1)}_{1},\frac{m}{m-1})}_{m,q^{(1)}}
with
q^{(1)}=\frac{d(m-1)}{d-2m}
. More precisely,

\frac{d}{q^{(1)}_{1}}+\frac{(m-1)(2+q^{(1)}_{m,d})}{m}=1+q^{(1)}_{m,d},\quad\text{where}\quad q_{m,d}^{(1)}=\frac{d(m-1)}{q^{(1)}}.

In fact, we can see that if
m>\frac{2d}{d+2}
, then
q^{(1)}>1
.
We denote

q^{(1)}=\frac{d(m-1)}{d-2m},\quad q^{(1)}_{1}=\frac{md}{d+2-3m},\quad\text{and}\quad q^{(1)}_{2}=\frac{m}{m-1}.

Now we derive an iterative formula.
Suppose that
q^{(k)}>1
is given and
\nabla c\in S^{(q^{(k)}_{1},q^{(k)}_{2})}_{m,q^{(k)}}
.
Then,

\rho
is upgraded as follows, thanks to Proposition
4.6
:

\rho\in L^{q^{(k)},\infty}_{x,t}\cap L^{\frac{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}{d-2},m+q^{(k)}-1}_{x,t}.

Next we show that

\nabla c\in L^{q^{(k+1)}_{1}}_{x}L^{\frac{m+q^{(k)}-1}{m-1}}_{t},\quad\text{for}\quad q^{(k+1)}_{1}=\frac{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}{d-3m+3-q^{(k)}}.

Indeed, reminding that
\rho\in L^{\frac{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}{d-2},m+q^{(k)}-1}_{x,t}
, it follows that for
q^{(k+1)}_{1}\geq\frac{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}{d-2}

\left\|\nabla c\right\|_{L^{q^{(k+1)}_{1}}_{x}}\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{t}(\nabla\Gamma*\rho)(\tau)d\tau\right\|_{L^{q^{(k+1)}_{1}}_{x}}\leq C\int_{0}^{t}(t-\tau)^{-\frac{d}{2}(\frac{d-2}{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}-\frac{1}{q^{(k+1)}_{1}})-\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\rho(\tau)\right\|_{L_{x}^{\frac{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}{d-2}}}\,d\tau.

Again via the Young’s inequality, we have

\left\|\nabla c\right\|_{L^{q^{(k+1)}_{1}}_{x}L^{\frac{m+q^{(k)}-1}{m-1}}_{t}}\leq C\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{\frac{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}{d-2}}_{x}L^{m+q^{(k)}-1}_{t}},\quad\text{for}\quad\frac{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}{d-2}\leq q^{(k+1)}_{1}\leq\frac{d(m+q^{(k)}-1)}{d-3m+3-q^{(k)}}.

Now we get

\nabla c\in S^{(q^{(k+1)}_{1},\frac{m+q^{(k)}-1}{m-1})}_{m,q^{(k+1)}}\quad\text{for}\quad q^{(k+1)}=\frac{d(m-1)q^{(k)}}{d-2m+2-2q^{(k)}}.

Then it is direct that
q^{(k+1)}>q^{(k)}
, because this is equivalent to

\frac{d(m-1)}{d-2m+2-2q^{(k)}}>1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad q^{(k)}>d+1-\frac{m(d+2)}{2}.

We note that
q^{(1)}>1
as long as
m>\frac{2d}{d+2}
.
We stop this process until
d-3m+3-q^{(k)}\leq 0
(it can be achieved in a finite step, since

q^{(k)}
is strictly increasing and it cannot converge).
Since
q^{(k)}\geq d-3m+3
, we note that

\frac{m+q^{(k)}-1}{m-1}\geq\frac{d-2m+2}{m-1}\geq 2,

for
m\leq\frac{d+2}{4}
. This is true for
m\leq\frac{2(d-1)}{d}
because
\frac{2(d-1)}{d}<\frac{d+4}{4}
, which is equivalent to
d^{2}-4d+8>0
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
2.21
.

Appendix A
Hölder continuity of homogeneous PME

Here we concern the homogeneous form of (
1.1
)

\partial_{t}u=\Delta u^{m}\ \text{ in }\ Q_{T}\quad\text{ for }\ m>1,

(A.1)

for
Q_{T}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,T]
,
d\geq 2
,
0<T<\infty
with a nonnegative initial data
u(x,0)=u_{0}(x)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In
[
11
,
12
]
, Caffarelli and Friedman proved the modulus of continuity of
u
in the entire half space, if
u_{0}(x)
is Hölder continuous. In
[
23
,
22
]
, DiBenedetto and Friedman determined Hölder exponent and constants quantitatively in the interior only depending upon the data. For
d=1
, the Hölder exponent is known explicitly as
\min\{1,\frac{1}{m-1}\}
by Aronson
[
3
,
4
]
.

The Hölder continuity up to initial boundary is known, for exmaple, see
[
25
, Section 6]
, as the Hölder exponent is depending upon the data and the modulus of continuity of the initial data. More specifically, for
u_{0}(x)\in C^{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, a bounded nonnegative weak solution of (
A.1
) is uniformly Hölder continuous up to the initial boundary with the exponent
\alpha=\alpha(m,d,\alpha_{0})\in(0,1)
and the coefficient constant
c=c(m,d,\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\bar{Q}_{T})})
. For more systemetic arguments, we refer
[
26
,
24
]
for parabolic
p
-Laplace equation and for both
p
-Laplace and porous medium equations.

Although the uniform Hölder continuity of a bounded nonnegative weak solution of (
A.1
) is well-known, the dependency of the Hölder exponent with respect to the initial Hölder exponent seems not specified from previous works mentioned above. Therefore, our aim is to more clearly demonstrate the relation of interior and initial Hölder regularity. More precisely, in Theorem
5.2
, we clarify the dependencies of uniform Hölder exponent as the minimum of the intertior Hölder exponent and given Hölder exponent of the initial data, that is,
\alpha=\min\{\alpha^{\ast},\alpha_{0}\}
where
\alpha^{\ast}=\alpha^{\ast}(m,d)
is determined from the interior Hölder result in Theorem
A.1
and
\alpha_{0}\in(0,1)
is given from
u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha_{0}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
To prove Theorem
5.2
, we mainly modify arguments, the boundary Hölder continuity of parabolic
p
-Laplace equations up to the initial time, by DiBenedetto in
[
26
, Theorem III]
. Also typical arguments, for example, in
[
25
,
23
,
17
,
33
]
, are used to prove local Hölder continuity of (
A.1
) quantitatively.

Let
K_{r}(x_{0})
denote the
d-
dimensional cube centered at
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and wedge
2r
for
r>0
, that is,

K_{r}(x_{0}):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|x^{i}-x_{0}^{i}|<r\},\quad\text{for}\quad x=(x^{1},\cdots,x^{d}).

Besides let us denote two types of parabolic cyliners in
Q_{T}
, for
r>0
and
0<t_{0}<t_{0}+r^{2}\leq T
,

Q_{r}:=K_{r}(x_{0})\times(t_{0},t_{0}+r^{2}]\quad\text{and}\quad Q_{r}^{0}:=K_{r}(x_{0})\times[0,r^{2}].

First, we introduce the interior Hölder continuity results in
[
23
,
22
,
25
,
24
]
. When the divergence form of drift term is combined, we refer results in
[
17
,
33
]
.

Theorem A.1

Let
u
be a weak solution of (
A.1
) in
Q_{1}\subset Q_{T}
. Then there exist
\alpha^{\ast}=\alpha^{\ast}(m,d)\in(0,1)
and
c=c(m,d,\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{1})})
, such that,
u

is uniformly Hölder continuous in
Q_{1/2}
with the exponent
\alpha^{\ast}
and the coefficient
c
; that is,
\|u\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha^{\ast}}(Q_{1/2})}\leq c
.

The main theorem is the following: the Hölder continuity up to the initial time.

Theorem A.2

Let
u
be a weak solution of (
A.1
) in
Q_{1}^{0}\subset Q_{T}
with

u_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha_{0}}(K_{1})
. Then there exists
\tilde{\alpha}=\min\{\alpha_{0},\alpha^{\ast}\}
where
\alpha^{\ast}=\alpha^{\ast}(m,d)\in(0,1)
is given in Theorem
A.1
, and
c=c(m,d,\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{1}^{0})})
, such that,
u
is uniformly Hölder continuous in
Q_{1/2}^{0}
with the exponent
\alpha
and the coefficient
c
; that is,
\|u\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}(Q_{1/2}^{0})}\leq c
.

Theorem
5.2
is a direct consequence of Theorem
A.1
and Theorem
A.2
.

The proof of Theorem
5.2
: The global Hölder estimate up to
t=0
, is given by the standard covering arguments based on both interior and boundary estimates in Theorem
A.1
and Theorem
A.2
.

It remains to prove Theorem
A.2
.
In the first subsection, we provide energy and logarithmic type estimates up to the initial time which are fundamental to carry further arguments in the following subsection to prove Theorem
A.2
.

Energy estimates

In this section, we apply the idea in
[
26
, Section II.4.iii]
and local energy estimates in
[
17
, Proposition 4.1 & 4.2]
to obtain energy estimates of (
5.2
) up to the initial time.

We consider a weak solution of (
A.1
) that takes the initial data
u_{0}
such that

\left(u_{h}(\cdot,0)-u_{0}{}\right)_{\pm}\to 0,\quad\text{as }h\to 0\quad\text{in $L^{2}_{\loc}$ sense,}

(A.2)

where
u_{h}
indicates the Stekelov average that

u_{h}(x,t)=\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}u(x,\tau)\,d\tau,\quad\text{for all}\quad 0\leq t\leq T-h,

which converges to
u
as
h\to 0
.

For some
k>0
, let us denote
(u-k)_{+}=\max\{u-k,0\}
and
(u-k)_{-}=\max\{k-u,0\}
.

Proposition A.3

Let
u
be a nonnegative bounded weak solution of (
A.1
). For
r>0
,
0<t<T
, suppose that
\zeta
is a smooth cut-off function in
K_{r}(x_{0})\times[0,t]
, which is
0\leq\zeta\leq 1
, independent of
t
, and vanishing on the lateral boundary
\partial K_{r}(x_{0})
.
Let
\mu_{\pm}
be positive constants such that

\mu_{+}\geq\esssup_{K_{r}(x_{0})\times[0,t]}u\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{-}\leq\essinf_{K_{r}(x_{0})\times[0,t]}u.

Moreover, for every level
k>0
, suppose that

\begin{cases}\mu_{+}-k>\esssup_{K_{r}(x_{0})}u_{0},&\quad\text{for}\quad(u-\mu_{+}-k)_{+},\\
\mu_{-}+k<\essinf_{K_{r}(x_{0})}u_{0},&\quad\text{for}\quad(u-\mu_{-}+k)_{-}.\end{cases}

(A.3)

Then the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq t}\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}\left(u(\cdot,\tau)-\mu_{\pm}\pm k\right)_{\pm}^{2}\zeta^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle+m\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}u^{m-1}\left|\nabla\left(u-\mu_{\pm}\pm k\right)_{\pm}\right|^{2}\zeta^{2}\,dx\,d\tau

(A.4)

\displaystyle\leq 16m\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}u^{m-1}\left(u-\mu_{\pm}\pm k\right)_{\pm}^{2}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}\,dx\,d\tau.

Moreover, it holds that, for any
\tau\in(0,t]
,

\displaystyle\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}\Psi_{\pm}^{2}(u(\cdot,\tau))\zeta^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle+2m\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}u^{m-1}|\nabla u|^{2}\Psi_{\pm}\left|\Psi^{\prime}_{\pm}(u)\right|^{2}\zeta^{2}\,dx\,d\tau

(A.5)

\displaystyle\leq 16m\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}u^{m-1}\Psi_{\pm}^{2}(u)|\nabla\zeta|^{2}\,dx\,d\tau,

where, for
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\Psi_{\pm}(u)=\ln^{+}\left[\frac{k}{(1+\delta)k-\left(u-\mu_{\pm}\pm k\right)_{\pm}}\right].

Proof.

A weak solution formulation of (
A.1
) rephrased in terms of Stekelov averages is given

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}\left[\partial_{t}u_{h}\varphi+\nabla u_{h}^{m}\nabla\varphi\right]\,dx\,dt=0,

for all
0<t<T-h
and for all
\varphi\in W_{o}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}_{x}(\Omega)
,
\varphi\geq 0
(see
[
26
, Section II.2.i]
).

To obtain (
A.4
), we test
\varphi=\pm 2\left(u_{h}-\mu_{\pm}\pm k\right)_{\pm}\zeta^{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}\partial_{t}u_{h}\varphi\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}\partial_{t}\left[\left(u_{h}-\mu_{\pm}\pm k\right)_{\pm}\zeta\right]^{2}\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})\times\{t\}}\left(u_{h}-\mu_{\pm}\pm k\right)_{\pm}^{2}\zeta^{2}\,dx\,dt-\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}\left(u_{h}(\cdot,0)-\mu_{\pm}\pm k\right)_{\pm}^{2}\zeta^{2}\,dx\,dt,

in which the second term vanishes as
h\to 0
because of (
A.2
) and (
A.3
). We skip the computation of
\iint\nabla u_{h}^{m}\nabla\varphi\,dx\,dt
because it is similar to
[
17
, Proposition 4.1]
. The combination of above estimates yields (
A.4
).

Now to obtain (
A.5
), we note that, from the definition of
\Psi_{\pm}(u)
, it follows that

\Psi_{\pm}(u)=0\quad\text{whenever $(u-\mu_{\pm}\pm k)_{\pm}=0$. }

Then (
A.3
) provides that

\int_{K_{r}(x_{0})}\Psi^{2}(u_{h})(x,0)\zeta^{2}\,dx\to 0,\quad\text{as $h\to 0$}.

Therefore, (
A.5
) follows by carrying almost the same computations as in
[
17
, Proposition 4.2]
.

Proof of Theorem
A.2
.

Here, we prove Theorem
A.2
by modifying methodologies in
[
26
, Section III]
.
Because of translation invariant property, we assume
x_{0}=0
without loss of generality. First, we construct a parabolic cyliner

Q_{2R,R^{2-\epsilon}}=K_{2R}(0)\times[0,R^{2-\epsilon}],\quad\text{for}\quad R>0,

(A.6)

where
\epsilon=\epsilon(m)
is a positive number determined later. Moreover, let us set

\mu_{+}:=\esssup_{Q_{2R,R^{2-\epsilon}}}u,\qquad\mu_{-}:=\essinf_{Q_{2R,R^{2-\epsilon}}}u,\quad\text{and}\quad\omega:=\essosc_{Q_{2R,R^{2-\epsilon}}}u=\mu_{+}-\mu_{-}.

(A.7)

Now we construct the intrinsic parabolic cylinder such that

Q_{R,a_{0}R^{2}}:=K_{R}(0)\times[0,a_{0}R^{2}],\quad\text{where}\quad\frac{1}{a_{0}}=\left(\frac{\omega}{4}\right)^{m-1}.

These parabolic boxes are lying on the bottom of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

We establish the decay of oscillation in the next proposition, which is similar to
[
26
, Proposition III.11.1]
.

Proposition A.4

There exist constants
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(m,d,\epsilon)\in(0,1)
for
\epsilon
given in (
A.6
),
\eta_{1}=\eta_{1}(m,d)\in(0,1)
, and
\eta_{2}=\eta_{2}(m,d)\in(0,1)
which satisfy the following.
Let us construct the sequences with
R_{0}=R
and
\omega_{0}=\omega
:

R_{n}={\eta_{2}}^{n}R,\quad\omega_{n+1}=\max\{\eta_{1}\omega_{n},\,4R_{n}^{\epsilon_{0}}\},\quad n=1,2,\ldots.

(A.8)

Also, construct the family of intrinsic parabolic cylinders:

Q_{n}:=K_{R_{n}}\times[0,\left(\frac{\omega_{n}}{4}\right)^{1-m}R_{n}^{2}],\quad n=1,2,\ldots.

that satisfies
Q_{n+1}\subset Q_{n}
.
Then the following holds: for all
n=0,1,2,\ldots
,

\essosc_{Q_{n}}u\leq\max\{\omega_{n},\,2\essosc_{K_{R_{n}}}u_{0}\}.

(A.9)

Proof.

For any
R>0
, let us start from
Q_{0}:=Q_{2R,R^{2-\epsilon}}
in (
A.6
) where
\epsilon\in(0,1)
to be determined later.
For
\mu_{\pm}
and
\omega
given in (
A.7
), we construct intrinsically scaled parabolic cylinders

Q_{R}^{\omega}:=K_{R}(0)\times[0,\left(\frac{\omega}{4}\right)^{1-m}R^{2}].

Then there are two cases: either

Q_{R}^{\omega}\subset Q_{0}\quad\text{which gives}\quad\omega>4R^{\frac{\epsilon}{m-1}},

(A.10)

or

Q_{0}\subset Q_{R}^{\omega}\quad\text{which gives}\quad\omega\leq 4R^{\frac{\epsilon}{m-1}}.

(A.11)

If (
A.11
) holds, then (
A.8
) follows directly with
\epsilon_{0}=\frac{\epsilon}{m-1}
.

Now let us assume (
A.10
) and set

\mu^{+}_{0}=\esssup_{K_{R}}u_{0},\quad\mu^{-}_{0}=\essinf_{K_{R}}u_{0},\quad\omega_{0}=\essosc_{K_{R}}u_{0}=\mu^{+}_{0}-\mu^{-}_{0}.

Then there are two inequalities to cosider:

\mu^{+}-\frac{\omega}{4}\leq\mu^{+}_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\mu^{-}+\frac{\omega}{4}\geq\mu^{-}_{0}.

(A.12)

If both inequalities in (
A.12
) hold, then the subtraction of the second from the first gives

\essosc_{Q_{R}^{\omega}}u\leq 2\essosc_{K_{R}}u_{0},

which holds (
A.9
) and there is nothing to prove.

If the second inequality in (
A.12
) is violated, then we choose
k=\frac{\omega}{4}
and it gives

\mu^{-}+k<\mu_{0}^{-},

which satisfies (
A.3
). Therefore, we have energy estimates (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) for
(u-\mu^{-}-k)_{-}
. With these estimates, we are able to carry the quantitative method such as the expansion of positivity and DeGiorgi iteration as in
[
17
]
. We obtain that there exists
\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(m,d)\in(0,1)
such that

\essinf_{Q_{R/8}^{\omega/4}}u\geq\mu_{-}+\delta_{1}\omega,\quad\text{for}\quad Q_{R/8}^{\omega/4}:=K_{R/8}\times[0,\left(\frac{\omega}{4}\right)^{1-m}\left(\frac{R}{8}\right)^{2}].

If the first inequality in (
A.12
) fails, then we have (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) for
(u-\mu^{+}+k)_{+}
because (
A.3
) holds
k=\frac{\omega}{4}
. Then again the same arguments in
[
17
]
deduce that there exists
\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}(m,d)\in(0,1)
such that

\esssup_{Q_{R/8}^{\omega/4}}u\leq\mu_{+}-\delta_{2}\omega.

From above, when (
A.12
) fails, we choose
\eta_{1}=\max\{1-\delta_{1},\ 1-\delta_{2}\}
to obtain

\essosc_{Q_{R/8}^{\omega/4}}u\leq\eta_{1}\omega,

which parallels to Lemma III.11.1 in
[
26
]
.

Finally, we construct nested and shrinking family of parabolic cylinders. First, let us set

{\eta_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{8}\,{\eta_{1}}^{\frac{m-1}{2}},\quad\text{for}\quad{\eta_{1}}=\max\{1-\delta_{1},\ 1-\delta_{2}\}\in(0,1).

(A.13)

This provides

Q_{1}:=Q_{{\eta_{2}}R}^{{\eta_{1}}\omega/4}\subset Q_{R/8}^{\omega/4}\subset Q_{R}^{\omega/4}\subset Q_{R}^{\omega/4}=:Q_{0}.

Then by repeating the same iteration, we complete the proof.

From Proposition
A.4
, we derive the Hölder continuity in the next lemma.

Lemma A.5

Let
u
be a nonnegative weak solution of (
A.1
) with
u_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then there exist
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(m)\in(0,1)
,
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}(m,d)\in(0,1)
for
i=1,2
, and
c=c(m,d)>1
, such that, for any
R>0
and
0<r\leq R
, it holds

\essosc_{Q_{r}^{0}}u\leq c\left(\omega+R^{\epsilon_{0}}+R^{\alpha_{0}}\right)\left(\frac{r+(\omega/4)^{\frac{m-1}{2}}r}{R}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}},

for
\tilde{\alpha}=\min\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\epsilon_{0}/2\}
.

Proof.

For
{\eta_{1}},{\eta_{2}}\in(0,1)
given in Proposition
A.4
, let us set
R_{n}={\eta_{2}}^{n}R
and
\omega_{n}={\eta_{1}}^{n}\omega
. For any
0<\rho\leq R
, there exist nonnegative integers
k
and
l
such that

R_{k+1}<r\leq R_{k}

(A.14)

and

\left(\frac{\omega_{l+1}}{4}\right)^{1-m}R_{l+1}^{2}<r^{2}\leq\left(\frac{\omega_{l}}{4}\right)^{1-m}R_{l}^{2},

(A.15)

which means
Q_{r}
belongs to either
Q_{k}
or
Q_{l}
. Therefore, by Proposition
A.4
, it implies

\essosc_{Q_{r}}u\leq\max\{\omega_{k},\ \omega_{l}\},

and, moreover, it gives (because
\essosc_{K_{R_{n}}}u_{0}\leq R_{n}^{\alpha_{0}}
by the Hölder continuity of
u_{0}
)

\omega_{n+1}\leq{\eta_{1}}\omega_{n}+4R_{n}^{\epsilon_{0}}+2R_{n}^{\alpha_{0}}.

First, we deduce from the iteration that

\omega_{n}\leq{\eta_{1}}^{n}\omega+4\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\eta_{1}}^{n-i}{\eta_{2}}^{\epsilon_{0}(i-1)}\right)R^{\epsilon_{0}}+2\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\eta_{1}}^{n-i}{\eta_{2}}^{\alpha_{0}(i-1)}\right)R^{\alpha_{0}}=I_{n}+II_{n}+III_{n}.

Then we consider two cases separately.

If (
A.14
) holds, then we deduce the following, from the LHS of (
A.14
),

{\eta_{2}}^{k+1}<\frac{r}{R}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad k+1<\log_{{\eta_{2}}}r/R=\log_{{\eta_{1}}}(r/R)^{\alpha_{1}},\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha_{1}=\left|\log_{{\eta_{2}}}{\eta_{1}}\right|.

Therefore, we easily obtain

{\eta_{1}}^{k}<{\eta_{1}}^{-1}\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\alpha_{1}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad I_{k}\leq{\eta_{1}}^{-1}\omega\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\alpha_{1}}.

(A.16)

Recalling (
A.13
), without loss of generality, let us fix

{\eta_{2}}={\eta_{1}}^{m-1}<\frac{1}{8}{\eta_{1}}^{\frac{m-1}{2}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad{\eta_{1}}={\eta_{2}}^{\frac{1}{m-1}}.

(A.17)

Then now we observe that

II_{k}=4\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\eta_{1}}^{k-i}{\eta_{2}}^{\epsilon_{0}(i-1)}\right)R^{\epsilon_{0}}=4\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\eta_{2}}^{\frac{1}{m-1}(k-i)}{\eta_{2}}^{\epsilon_{0}(i-1)}\right)R^{\epsilon_{0}}\leq 4\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\eta_{2}}^{\epsilon_{0}(k-1)}\right)R^{\epsilon_{0}},

by choosing
\epsilon_{0}=\min\{1/2,\ 1/(m-1)\}
.
Therefore, it yields

II_{k}\leq 4{\eta_{2}}^{-\epsilon_{0}}k({\eta_{2}}^{k}R)^{\epsilon_{0}}\leq 4{\eta_{2}}^{-\epsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{\epsilon_{0}/2}\left(\frac{r}{{\eta_{2}}}\right)^{\epsilon_{0}}=4{\eta_{2}}^{-2\epsilon_{0}}R^{\epsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\epsilon_{0}/2},

(A.18)

by (
A.14
) and by choosing
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

k<{\eta_{2}}^{-k\epsilon_{0}/2}\leq\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{\epsilon_{0}/2}.

(A.19)

The second inequality of (
A.19
) comes form the RHS of (
A.14
), and the first inequality of (
A.19
) is true in general by choosing
\frac{1}{{\eta_{2}}}
large enough.
Let us fix

\tilde{\alpha}=\min\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\epsilon_{0}/2\},

(A.20)

and then it follows

\displaystyle III_{k}

\displaystyle=2\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\eta_{1}}^{k-i}{\eta_{2}}^{\alpha_{0}(i-1)}\right)R^{\alpha_{0}}\leq 2\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\eta_{2}}^{\epsilon_{0}(k-i)}{\eta_{2}}^{\alpha_{0}(i-1)}\right)R^{\alpha_{0}}

(A.21)

\displaystyle\leq 2{\eta_{2}}^{-\epsilon_{0}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\eta_{2}}^{(\epsilon_{0}-\tilde{\alpha})(k-i+1)}\right)({\eta_{2}}^{k}R)^{\tilde{\alpha}}<\frac{2{\eta_{2}}^{-2\epsilon_{0}}}{{\eta_{2}}^{-\epsilon_{0}/2}-1}R^{\alpha_{0}}\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}},

because, by the LHS of (
A.14
),

\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\eta_{2}}^{(\epsilon_{0}-\tilde{\alpha})(k-i+1)}<\frac{1}{{\eta_{2}}^{\alpha_{0}-\epsilon_{0}}-1}\leq\frac{1}{{\eta_{2}}^{-\epsilon_{0}/2}-1},\quad\text{and}\quad{\eta_{2}}^{-\tilde{\alpha}}<{\eta_{2}}^{-\epsilon_{0}}.

Finally, the summation of (
A.16
), (
A.18
), (
A.21
), and the choice of
\alpha_{0}
in (
A.20
) imply that

\omega_{l}\leq{\eta_{1}}^{-1}\omega\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\alpha_{1}}+4{\eta_{2}}^{-2\epsilon_{0}}R^{\epsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\epsilon_{0}/2}+\frac{2{\eta_{2}}^{-2\epsilon_{0}}}{{\eta_{2}}^{-\epsilon_{0}/2}-1}R^{\alpha_{0}}\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}}\leq c\left(\omega+R^{\epsilon_{0}}+R^{\alpha_{0}}\right)\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}}.

If (
A.15
) hold, then let us rewrite (
A.15
) in the following:

\left({\eta_{1}}^{1-m}{\eta_{2}}^{2}\right)^{l+1}R^{2}<\left(\frac{\omega}{4}\right)^{m-1}r^{2}\ (=:\tilde{r}^{2})\leq\left({\eta_{1}}^{1-m}{\eta_{2}}^{2}\right)^{l}R^{2}.

(A.22)

Let us set

\tilde{{\eta_{2}}}={\eta_{1}}^{\frac{1-m}{2}}{\eta_{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{r}^{2}=\left(\frac{\omega}{4}\right)^{m-1}r^{2}.

Then (
A.22
) is now simplified as

\tilde{{\eta_{2}}}^{l+1}R<\tilde{r}\leq\tilde{{\eta_{2}}}^{l}R.

Without loss of generality, assume that
\tilde{r}\leq R
.
Moreover, the choice of
{\eta_{2}}
as in (
A.17
) gives

{\eta_{1}}^{1-m}{\eta_{2}}^{2}={\eta_{1}}^{m-1}={\eta_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{{\eta_{2}}}={\eta_{2}}^{3/2}.

Therefore, the first inequality in (
A.22
) yields

l+1<\log_{{\eta_{1}}}\left(\tilde{r}/R\right)^{\alpha_{2}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad I_{l}\leq{\eta_{1}}^{-1}\omega\left(\frac{\tilde{r}}{R}\right)^{\alpha_{2}},\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha_{2}=\left|\log_{\tilde{{\eta_{2}}}}{\eta_{1}}\right|.

Then we carry similar analysis for
II_{l}
and
III_{l}
as handling
II_{k}
in (
A.18
) and
III_{k}
as in (
A.21
) with
\tilde{\alpha}=\min\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2},\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2}\}
to have

\omega_{l}\leq{\eta_{1}}^{-1}\omega\left(\frac{\tilde{r}}{R}\right)^{\alpha_{2}}+4\tilde{{\eta_{2}}}^{-2\epsilon_{0}}R^{\epsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{\tilde{r}}{R}\right)^{\epsilon_{0}/2}+\frac{2{\eta_{2}}^{-2\epsilon_{0}}}{{\eta_{2}}^{-\epsilon_{0}/2}-1}R^{\alpha_{0}}\left(\frac{\tilde{r}}{R}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}}\leq c\left(\omega+R^{\epsilon_{0}}+R^{\alpha_{0}}\right)\left(\frac{\tilde{r}}{R}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}}.

Therefore, we complete the proof by choosing
\tilde{\alpha}=\min\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2}\}
and by combining all estimates above.

Once we have Lemma
A.5
, Theorem
A.2
follows from standard computations (refer
[
26
,
17
,
33
]
).

Embedding

The following figures illustrate strategies of applying embedding arguments in the temporal varaiable in the second part os Theorem
2.9
,
2.13
, and
2.16
.
Unfortunately, embedding arguments in spatial variables do not work because
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is unbounded. If spatial embedding arguments are applicable, then, for example in Figure 4-(e), it gives a way to include the region
\mathcal{R}(\textbf{aAF})
by searching
(q_{1}^{\ast},q_{2})
in
\mathcal{R}(\textbf{ABCDEF})
by decreasing
q_{1}^{\ast}<q_{1}
for some
q^{\ast}\in(1,q)
.
We are preparing a parallel paper constructing the existence results of (
1.1
) in
\Omega\times(0,T]
for
\Omega
bounded in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
